# ATCS6 Desired Design Model

This document defines the target refactor contracts and changed cross-component behavior for the current ATCS6 codebase.
It is intended to guide an engineer refactoring the existing implementation, not to replace the code as a complete standalone implementation spec.
Unchanged entity-local fields, helper internals, and serialization details continue to follow the current code unless this document states otherwise.
If current code conflicts with a changed contract stated here, this document wins.

Implementation note: use `design_new_implementation_index.md` as the compact working map during coding. This file remains the authoritative source for changed contracts.

## Authoring Rules

These rules govern every edit to this document. Reviewers must reject any patch that violates them.

1. **This document is v1. There is no "removed in v1," "legacy," "deprecated," or "no longer used" prose anywhere in this file.** If a field, method, state, or flag is not part of v1 it must not appear here at all. The absence of a row in a schema table is itself the statement that the thing does not exist. Any patch that introduces tombstone language ("removed in v1," "no longer used," "kept for compatibility," "deprecated," etc.) is incomplete and must be rejected.

2. **No cross-reference drift.** When any rule changes — ownership, payload field, state transition, callback signature, latch consumer, recovery gate — every site in the document that references the old rule must be swept and updated in the same edit. A patch that updates only one section is incomplete. Reviewers must reject any patch that leaves divergent restatements of the same rule anywhere in the document.

3. **One canonical home per concept.** Each shared schema, latch, predicate, and helper has exactly one canonical definition site. All other sites reference it by name. If two sites describe the same concept differently, the patch is incomplete.

4. **Latches are described in the canonical Latch Table.** Every latch in the document must appear in the Latch Table (in the Core Runtime Loop section) with: writer phase + entity, consumer phase + entity, the "consumed by the last entity that uses it in the same 3-phase period" rule, and any multi-tick survival conditions. Latch behavior described elsewhere must cross-reference the table, not restate its rules.

5. **Phase boundaries are described in the canonical Phase Table.** The phase entity-chain table (in the Core Runtime Loop section) is the only place that defines which entity acts in which phase. All other sections cross-reference it by phase name and entity.

6. **No single-use-case branches in shared contracts.** If a workflow step is written as a per-case branch, it must either be expressed as a general mechanism or explicitly justified as the only case the general mechanism cannot cover.

## Scope

Primary files reviewed:

- `simulation.py`
- `train.py`
- `train_controller.py`
- `train_physics.py`
- `railway_platform.py`
- `station.py`
- `block.py`
- `block_manager.py`
- `path.py`
- `path_dispatcher.py`
- `route_executor.py`
- `train_route.py`
- `track.py`
- `switch.py`
- `network.py`
- `speed_profile.py`
- `block_power.py`

Terminology:

- `path`: a dispatcher-managed reserved movement authority between blocks.
- `route`: a higher-level travel plan made of `stop` / `waypoint` / `dwell` tasks that ultimately resolve to concrete block targets.
- `route run`: the per-train runtime instance of an assigned route. It references one shared `Route` definition but owns that train's mutable execution state: waypoint cursors, the current resolved target status, dwell timing, and active-path correlation. Current-code implementation may still keep the class name `RouteExecutionState`, but the canonical concept is `RouteRun`.
- `block`: an occupancy and reservation unit.
- `track tile` / `TrackSegment`: one physical rail tile on the grid, identified by its `(x, y)` coordinate and local 6-bit geometry. Despite the legacy class name, a `TrackSegment` is not a collection of tiles and is not a block; a `Block` owns an ordered collection of these tile IDs.
- `platform`: the concrete physical stopping place on the track. Dispatcher targets are always expressed against a concrete block traversal frame, never against a logical station group; when a waypoint resolves through station/platform data, the platform supplies the linked blocks and traversal context used to derive that block target.
- `station`: a logical grouping of one or more platforms used only for route-level stop selection. When a route targets a station-level stop, `RouteExecutor` must first resolve one concrete platform for that attempt and then derive the dispatcher request contract from that resolved platform.

## Design Direction

**Ownership split**

- `TrainController` should own live operational state: ATC/ATO/manual mode, handle position, future manual `power_percent`-mode inputs, and the canonical live lookahead data series. "Target speed" and "speed-limit reason" should not be stored as standalone fields anywhere; they should be the value and reason metadata at position 0 of the lookahead series.
- `TrainController` should also own live power/cab state: `power_on`, active cab selection, and any live direction-selection inputs tied to that active cab. After the runtime 1:1 `Train` <-> `TrainController` pairing is established, those values are not re-synced from `Train` on every tick.
- `Train` should own persistent/configuration state only: identity, consist, schedule/route assignment, and non-live train configuration. Initial live placement is set by the train-addition API flow: `SimulationController.add_train(...)` is the canonical owner of this API call, invoked from the frontend/client layer when a new train is introduced into the running simulation. This is an ownership change, not a new public request-shape requirement: the existing train-create payload shape (`id`, optional `position: {x, y}`, `direction`, `max_speed`, `length`, and other train configuration fields already accepted by the repo API) remains the frontend/API input unless separately changed by implementation. `SimulationController.add_train(...)` must create the `Train`, create or obtain the `TrainController`, derive the initial span from that placement/configuration payload, and write the initial `TrainController.current_span` before the first simulation tick. V1 does not accommodate the old setup where live placement, power/cab, or authority mirrors are restored directly from `Train` fields; bootstrap data for a v1 runtime must enter through `SimulationController.add_train(...)` or through explicit controller commands, and stale live mirrors are ignored or rejected rather than migrated into a second initialization path. Live position/span, live speed, ATO/ATC/path-runtime state, power/cab state, and operational state should not be mirrored on `Train`.
- `manual_target_speed` should be removed entirely. With the new handle plus power-percentage manual model there is no per-train target speed to store, so no compatibility shim is needed.
- Manual control should have two controller-owned submodes:
  - `simulation`: user drives with handle notches; the handle command flows to `TrainPhysics`, which integrates it into speed change over time.
  - `power_percent`: user drives with percentage power; the controller passes raw percentage power to `TrainPhysics`, which immediately converts it via train/profile calibration data such as `SpeedProfile.power_speed_curve` into both track voltage and the calibrated current speed when no braking override is active.
- `TrainPhysics` and `BlockPowerController` should not decide manual-mode policy. They should execute whichever command the controller produces and perform command-to-speed conversion and voltage emission.
- Percentage-power mapping data should live in `SpeedProfile.power_speed_curve` as part of train calibration and be read by physics.

## Layered Architecture

**Layered responsibilities**

- `PathDispatcher` should execute the route plan by acquiring future blocks, locking future switches, and managing movement authority. It should plan for the controller by providing the dispatcher-owned authority geometry and current authority-end block; the controller projects the actual braking target from that boundary using its safety-margin policy.
- `PathDispatcher` should remain the sole owner of dispatcher path records. `TrainController.received_path_plan` is only a pushed view of the currently active authority, not the source of truth.
- `PathDispatcher` should keep each path record keyed by `path_id` in a dispatcher-owned registry such as `paths_by_id[path_id]`.
- `PathDispatcher` should also keep a single active-authority index such as `active_path_id_by_train[train_id]`. Each train has at most one active authority path at a time, and the controller inbox mirrors only that newest active path.
- Cancelled or completed paths may remain in `paths_by_id[path_id]` for history after controller authority has been cleared, but they are not active authority and are never mirrored into `TrainController.received_path_plan`. In v1 there is no path-owned deferred-release flag. `PathDispatcher` owns only future authority resources: blocks and switches the train has not yet entered. When the train head enters a resource, that future reservation/lock is consumed and physical occupancy becomes resource-local physical state through dispatcher-mediated writes. Cancellation and completion release only not-yet-entered dispatcher-owned reservations and locks, clear controller authority, clear `active_path_id_by_train[train_id]`, and transition `Path.status` synchronously. Physical train motion after completion/cancellation is not blocked by retained path rows: `TrainPhysics` keeps moving the train according to live network geometry/state, and physical `occupied_by` state is cleared only by later controller-reported tail-vacate release events. `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` must reject a same-train request as its first step when that train already has an `ACTIVE` path, with no path ID allocation, switch acquisition, block reservation, path push, or switch-state mutation. Once the old path is no longer active, later creation is governed only by ordinary live resource conflicts.
- Controller progress reports identify the moving train by `train_id` only; they do not carry a caller-supplied `path_id`. `TrainController` owns the canonical stored span and speed. `TrainPhysics.tick()` computes the next physical span/speed plus the tile-level delta for that movement, then calls `TrainController.on_physics_updated(event_time, span_update)` once per crossed resource boundary and once more for the final no-boundary callback for that tick. `span_update` carries the new `TrainSpan`, the new `current_speed_kmh`, ordered `added_tiles`, ordered `removed_tiles`, and the traversal frame used for each added/removed tile. `TrainController.on_physics_updated(event_time, span_update)` atomically applies the new span/speed to controller-owned storage, interprets the tile delta against `TrainController.received_path_plan.resource_sequence` when authority exists plus canonical lookahead, and enqueues one ordered `ControllerProgressReport` into that train's Phase 3 mailbox. Phase 3 is the only place that drains those queued reports by calling `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)`. `PathDispatcher` resolves any active path from `active_path_id_by_train[train_id]`, decides whether reported resources belong to that path, and then commits active progress. After authority has been cleared, later physical release must not depend on a retained path row surviving: block release is driven by `block_id` plus `direction` through `BlockManager`, and switch release is driven by the vacated switch resource's train-owned physical occupancy/protection state.
- `TrainController` should execute the path plan by calculating ATC safety limits, ATO/manual commands, and an active handle or future power command. It should plan for physics by producing the active control command for the current mode.
- `TrainPhysics` should remain an executor only; it should not decide manual-mode policy.
- `PathDispatcher` pushes `path_id`, `resource_sequence`, and `authority_end_block_id` to the controller via `received_path_plan` whenever a path is created or extended. The controller brakes to the current authority boundary using its canonical safety margin and derives speed restrictions from lookahead over the network and dispatcher authority structure, not from per-block speed limits pushed as separate data. The requested target (`requested_destination_block_id`, `requested_destination_direction`) is stored on the dispatcher-owned `Path` record but is not included in the controller's `received_path_plan`. Dispatcher completion enforces the requested-target rule internally: the active path completes only when the live authority end block equals `Path.requested_destination_block_id` and the terminal authority traversal direction equals `Path.requested_destination_direction`; the controller does not see those fields.
- The dispatcher-owned path record should be the single source of truth for controller authority while it is active. Cancelling or replacing a path in `PathDispatcher` should be sufficient to give the controller a clean slate.

## Core Runtime Loop

**Phase structure**

### Per-train tick transaction

1. Advance `simulation_time`.
2. `RouteExecutor` owns the runnable-train ordering for the tick. `SimulationController.update(...)` advances time, calls `RouteExecutor.update(delta_seconds)` to refresh route-managed scheduling state, then passes the candidate train IDs to `RouteExecutor.order_runnable_trains(...)` and consumes the ordered list it returns. Route-managed trains are ordered by descending current shared `Route.priority`; priority values are integers where `9` is highest, `0` is lowest, and default priority is `5`. Trains with no current route-managed Phase 1 work, including one-off/manual trains, participate in the same ordering at effective priority `5`; a train that is powered off at the start of its transaction uses the same effective priority it would use when powered but skips Phase 1 because route planning cannot run before the command drain has had a chance to accept `TrainController.power_on_cmd()`. Ties use stable existing iteration order. `SimulationController` still owns executing each train transaction in that returned order. During each train transaction, `SimulationController.update(...)` calls `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(train_id, delta_seconds)` as the entire Phase 1 route-executor tick for that train before it calls `SimulationController._update_train(train, delta_seconds)`.
3. For each train in that order, run one full three-phase transaction to completion before starting the next train:
  - **Phase 1 - Setup (top-down planning for that train)**: `RouteExecutor` reconciles route state, handles any pending-arrival dwell entry, normalizes already-covered progress, resolves the current next target for that train only, and may call `PathDispatcher.create_path()` or `PathDispatcher.extend_path()` for that train only. After successful path creation, authority growth, or a successful `blocked_no_growth` target refresh, `PathDispatcher` pushes the canonical `received_path_plan` directly into that train's existing `TrainController`. Phase 1 may retry or loop locally in the same tick only while it stays inside Phase-1-owned procedures such as covered-waypoint normalization, target arming, candidate selection, and `create_path(...)` / `extend_path(...)` acquisition-and-rollback. In v1, if shared-route reconciliation, operator retargeting/skip, or a route/controller invariant check concludes that the current live path must be replaced, Phase 1 must not call `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` directly and must not keep a hidden desired-target override attached to the still-live path. Instead it latches `pending_cancel_request` for Phase 3, stops planning for that train for that tick, and waits for the ordinary Phase 3 cancel -> callback -> stopped recovery flow. It sets `pending_route_abort` only when the operator/admin intent is to remove the `RouteRun` rather than replan it. After authority cancellation has committed and the train reaches the appropriate stopped recovery point, the next eligible Phase 1 reruns ordinary covered-waypoint normalization plus the Phase-1 target-arm step from current physical progress and requests a brand-new path; for this fresh create/replan branch, eligible means the controller is `IDLE` or `STANDBY`, has no live authority, and has clean handoff state (`departure_requested == False`, `dwell_entry_requested == False`, `pending_cancel_request is None`, `pending_route_abort is None`, `pending_power_off == False`, `eb_active == False`, and `RouteRun.path_cancelled == False`).
  - **Phase 2 - Execute (controller + physics for that train)**: `TrainController.tick(...)` begins by draining that controller's queued operator commands. That command-drain substep runs after Phase 1 has completed for the train and before any power check, pre-physics lookahead consumption, ATC/ATO/manual decision, or physics movement. After the command drain and EB gate, `TrainController.tick(...)` consumes any Phase-1-to-Phase-2 handoffs (`dwell_entry_requested`, then `departure_requested`) before normal controller decisions. The same `TrainController.tick(...)` call then begins from the cached lookahead produced by the previous post-physics refresh when available, or computes that pre-physics lookahead on demand if the cache is empty. The command-drain substep may set `pending_power_off`, write `pending_cancel_request`, and immediately clear `ato_running` for accepted stopped power/cab/mode/EB commands, but it does not commit path completion or dispatcher cancellation directly. `TrainPhysics.tick(...)` then computes movement for that same train and must call `TrainController.on_physics_updated(...)` at least once for that tick.
  - **Phase 3 - Feedback and cleanup for that train**: `SimulationController` runs one general feedback/cleanup pass for that train. It first drains queued progress reports in order. That drain is the only normal place where physical entry/tail-vacate writes, current-resource advancement, route head-advance callbacks, and movement-derived deviation/fatal handling are committed. Route progress is callback-by-callback: each `RouteExecutor.handle_train_advanced(...)` call receives one reported resource advance and compares that single advance against the current next waypoint on the `RouteRun` directed route. If that waypoint is finished or passed by the callback's reported block/direction, the callback advances route progress for that one waypoint, clears the consumed armed target, and returns; the next progress callback is what discovers or tests the next waypoint. The callback may read pure route-mode waypoint progression and station/platform/block membership needed to test the current reported advance, but it must not arm future authority, must not write a new planning `resolved_targets` chain for the next leg, and must not call `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` or `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)`; Phase 1 remains the only owner of target arming and authority creation/growth. If a queued report cancels the active path, that movement-derived cancellation wins immediately. After all queued reports have been consumed, if an active path still exists for that train it performs the post-physics usable-authority stop check and, on success, samples the current system Unix timestamp as `event_time` before calling `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(train_id, event_time)`. After that normal progress/completion chance, if `Route.tasks[exec_state.current_waypoint_index].task_kind == "dwell"` and `TrainController.state == RUNNING_AT_STATION`, Phase 3 runs the dwell-complete timer check (reading the recorded arrival time from `exec_state.task_runs[current_waypoint_index].arrived_at`) and, when `RouteTask.should_depart(arrived_at, current_time)` is satisfied, marks the dwell task finished (sets `task_runs[current_waypoint_index].finished_at`), performs dwell-exit cleanup by calling `TrainController.finish_dwell()`, advances `current_waypoint_index`, and runs any terminal route-removal helper. Note: when EB interrupts dwell and is later released, dwell re-entry preserves the original `task_runs[dwell_index].arrived_at` (it is **not** reset on re-entry); if the dwell window has already elapsed during the EB cycle, `RouteTask.should_depart(...)` returns true on the next dwell-complete check and dwell exits immediately, which is the intended behavior. Dwell is a normal route step: while a run is pending dwell or dwelling, Phase 1 does not create or extend authority for that train; when dwell finishes, the task is marked finished and the next target is left unarmed for a later Phase 1 tick. After the dwell-complete cleanup step and before consuming `pending_cancel_request`, Phase 3 runs the **stopped-recovery cleanup**. This is a latch-gated check: it fires when `RouteRun.path_cancelled == True` or `TrainController.eb_released == True`, and the gating state is satisfied — `TrainController.state in {IDLE, STANDBY}`, `received_path_plan is None`, `eb_active == False`, `pending_power_off == False`. When the gate is met, Phase 3 clears `path_cancelled` (when set), clears `eb_released` (when set), and resets diagnostic `current_route_status.state` from `DEVIATED` to `RUNNING`, leaving the next leg unarmed for a later Phase 1 tick. The latches survive across ticks until the gating state is satisfied; this is what ensures correct multi-tick recovery after EB, cancellation, route-edit reconciliation, or any other path interruption. Only after that normal progress/completion/dwell/recovery chance has been handled does Phase 3 consume explicit latched teardown in this order: `pending_cancel_request`, `pending_route_abort`, then `pending_power_off`. Phase 3 is the only phase that calls `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)`, `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(...)`, or `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)`.
4. After all trains have completed their per-train transactions, update signals from blocks once for the tick.

- The three phases are therefore interleaved per train, not as three world-wide passes. One train's Phase 1/2/3 transaction fully commits before the next train's Phase 1 begins.
- Phase ownership in v1 is strict:
  - Phase 1 owns downward resolution and same-phase retries: pending-arrival dwell entry, route reconciliation, covered-waypoint normalization, next-target selection, `create_path(...)`, `extend_path(...)`, and ordinary departure decisions that do not require cross-phase cleanup.
  - Phase 2 owns controller-side observation and execution for that same train: the operator-command drain at the start of `TrainController.tick(...)`, pre-physics lookahead consumption or cache-miss construction, ATC/manual/ATO command generation, physics integration, and the regular post-physics lookahead refresh triggered by `TrainController.on_physics_updated(...)`. The **operator-command processing slot** is the first substep of `TrainController.tick(...)`; it is not a separate `SimulationController` drain method.
  - Phase 3 owns commit/cleanup, in this canonical order: (a) queued progress-report consumption including all movement-derived deviation handling, physical entry/tail-vacate writes, callback-by-callback route-progress checks against the current task; (b) normal stop-arrival/completion evaluation; (c) dwell-complete evaluation plus dwell-exit task-finish cleanup; (d) stopped-recovery cleanup (latch-gated: when `RouteRun.path_cancelled == True` or `TrainController.eb_released == True` and gating state is met — controller `IDLE`/`STANDBY`, `received_path_plan is None`, `eb_active == False`, `pending_power_off == False` — clear those latches and reset diagnostic `current_route_status.state` from `DEVIATED` to `RUNNING`); (e) explicit `pending_cancel_request` consumption; (f) explicit `pending_route_abort` consumption; (g) explicit `pending_power_off` consumption; (h) **phase-end clear**: clear consumed-but-stale `RouteRun.task_runs[i].resolved_target` entries and any other in-pass scratch state, so that next-tick logic begins from a clean slate. Latches written *during* this Phase 3 pass (e.g. by the cancellation callback in step e) are not consumed in the same pass; they wait for the next tick's Phase 3. Phase 3 must not arm a fresh planning target or create fresh authority; those wait for the next eligible Phase 1 for that train.

**Operator-command processing slot**

- Operator commands targeted at a train (`TrainController.power_on_cmd()`, `TrainController.set_cab(...)`, `TrainController.set_mode(...)`, `TrainController.set_manual_mode_type(...)`, `TrainController.set_handle(...)`, `TrainController.set_manual_power_percent(...)`, `TrainController.activate_emergency_cmd()`, `TrainController.release_emergency_cmd()`, `TrainController.power_off_cmd()`) are queued onto the targeted train's `TrainController` as they arrive from the API/UI layer. They are drained by `TrainController.tick(...)` as its first substep for that train, before pre-physics lookahead consumption and before any controller decision. For powered-on trains this substep runs after Phase 1 has completed planning; for powered-off trains Phase 1 is skipped and this Phase-2 tick entry still runs so `TrainController.power_on_cmd()` can be accepted. Operator intent is therefore reflected in the same tick's controller commands and physics step when a train is powered, while route planning after power-on waits for a later Phase 1 tick.
- Within that drain slot, queued commands run in the canonical order below so lower-urgency configuration is observed by higher-urgency commands on the same tick:
  1. `power_on_cmd()`
  2. `set_cab(...)`
  3a. `set_mode(...)`
  3b. `set_manual_mode_type(...)`
  4. `set_handle(...)` / `set_manual_power_percent(...)`
  5. `activate_emergency_cmd()`
  6. `release_emergency_cmd()`
  7. `power_off_cmd()`

  Inside slot 3, `set_mode(...)` always runs before `set_manual_mode_type(...)` because the latter requires `control_mode == "manual"` to accept.
- Each command's stopped-only / power-on / EB-not-latched / cab-selected preconditions are re-checked at the moment it is drained, against the controller state established by earlier commands in the same drain. A command that fails its preconditions is rejected (`success=False`) and does not block later commands in the same queue.
- Commands that write Phase-3 latches (`pending_cancel_request` from operator-issued and ATC-derived EB paths, `pending_route_abort` from explicit route-abort requests, `eb_released` from `release_emergency_cmd()`, `pending_power_off` from `power_off_cmd()`) write those latches during this tick-entry command drain or, for ATC-derived EB activation, from later inside the same `TrainController.tick()` call; the same train's later Phase 3 consumes them in the canonical Phase-3 latch-consumption order. External API/UI/admin operations must honor this same internal workflow: they enqueue controller commands or set the controller-owned Phase-3 handoff payloads and then let the per-train phase loop commit dispatcher/route/resource mutations. They must not call `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)`, clear controller authority, remove route runs, or release resources inline from the request handler. `TrainController.activate_emergency_cmd()` is the canonical operator-issued EB command; ATC overspeed escalation and no-authority insufficient-service-braking escalation can also raise EB from inside `TrainController.tick()`. Both paths perform the same controller-local effects (set `eb_active = True`, force handle to `EB`, clear `ato_running`, clear `departure_ready_path_id`) and, when a live path exists, write `pending_cancel_request` with `cancellation_kind=PathCancellationKind.EB` so Phase 3 commits that cancellation through the same dispatcher cancel-and-release path used by every other cancellation kind.
- API/UI command submission has a separate result from command execution. A request handler returns success once the command has been appended to the targeted controller's command queue, or once a direct Phase-3 handoff such as `pending_cancel_request` has been written; it does not block waiting for the later tick-entry drain. The real command result is produced when `TrainController.tick(...)` drains the command: each drained command emits one `command_result` notification through the runtime `NotificationCenter` (`command_id`, command name, `train_id`, `success`, optional `reason`, and `event_time`) for UI/API consumers. If a command fails its stopped-only, power, EB, cab, or mode preconditions at drain time, that later result is `success=False` with the diagnostic reason; the original enqueue response remains an accepted/pending submission. `TrainController.power_off_cmd()` is the exception to any generic EB-not-latched guard: it may be accepted with EB latched when the stopped-only shutdown precondition is satisfied.
- `TrainController.release_emergency_cmd()` is also latch-based. It may clear `eb_active` only after the EB-triggered path cancellation has already committed and live authority has been cleared. If `TrainController.pending_cancel_request` still contains an EB cancellation payload for the current live path, or `PathDispatcher.active_path_id_by_train[train_id]` still references the pre-EB live authority, `TrainController.release_emergency_cmd()` must reject or defer the release with a diagnostic command-result notification. On acceptance it transitions to the state-machine-derived stopped ready state, sets the generic `departure_confirmation_required = True` latch, and writes the controller-owned `eb_released = True` handoff for Phase 3. It must not call `RouteExecutor` directly and must not mutate `RouteRun` directly; route continuation/replanning is discovered by the normal Phase-3 stopped-recovery cleanup and the next eligible Phase 1 planning pass.

**Runtime notifications**

- `NotificationCenter` is the only runtime notification owner in v1. It is exposed through `SimulationController.notification_center` and is responsible for storing, sequencing, and projecting runtime notifications to frontend/API consumers. Controllers, dispatcher, route executor, and admin surfaces emit notifications by calling this class; they do not keep component-local frontend notification queues.
- Every notification carries at minimum `notification_id`, `event_time`, `source`, `kind`, `severity`, optional `train_id`, optional `path_id`, and a structured `payload`. `NotificationCenter` assigns monotonically increasing `notification_id` values in emission order and is the only component that decides retention, read/ack state, and frontend delivery mechanics such as polling, streaming, or websocket fanout.
- Command execution results are notifications with `kind = "command_result"` and payload `{command_id, command_name, success, reason}`. Path cancellation/completion diagnostics, fatal runtime failures, route-run errors, and operator-visible planning failures also flow through `NotificationCenter`; the originating component still owns the underlying state mutation, while `NotificationCenter` owns only the user-facing event record and delivery surface.
- Request handlers must not read controller internals to discover later semantic command failures. They return accepted/pending for enqueue or latch writes, then frontend/API consumers observe later results through `NotificationCenter`.

**Phase 1 departure-request writes (manual + ATO)**

- For both manual and ATO modes, Phase 1 writes `TrainController.departure_requested` on the first eligible Phase 1 pass that observes live authority and clean handoff state, regardless of whether the driver has already started rolling manually. This guarantees that route-managed manual operation also goes through the canonical depart-acceptance path; manual driver pre-depart movement is allowed (and protected by ATC) but the route-side "depart accepted, route progress can complete a stop" gate (`departure_ready_path_id`) is not skipped just because the train moved early. If `TrainController.depart()` rejects the handoff (e.g. EB latched, no cab), Phase 1 may re-request on the next eligible tick.

**Phase handoff rule**

- A phase may retry immediately only when every operation stays inside that same phase.
- If logic in one phase needs another phase to mutate live runtime state, it must write a per-train latch or queue entry instead of calling across phases directly.
- The per-train Phase 3 mailbox / flag table is controller-owned in v1. Its canonical home is `TrainController.phase3_mailbox` (or equivalent controller-owned fields when implemented without a nested object).
- The v1 mailbox contains:
  - `queued_progress_reports`
  - `pending_cancel_request`
  - `pending_route_abort`
  - `eb_released`
  - `pending_power_off` (controller-owned latch consumed by Phase 3)
- `pending_cancel_request` is an optional payload, not a bare boolean. It is the controller-owned handoff for explicit non-movement **authority cancellation** that must be committed in Phase 3, including route-edit/replan revocation, operator/admin path cancellation, and Phase-2 EB requests. Ordinary movement-derived deviation does not use this latch; it is committed inline while Phase 3 drains queued progress reports. The payload's canonical fields are `cancellation_kind`, `reason`, and optional `expected_path_id` for diagnostics. It does not decide whether the `RouteRun` should be removed. If multiple same-tick writers set `pending_cancel_request`, the most recent payload wins; Phase 3 still resolves the actual live path from `PathDispatcher.active_path_id_by_train[train_id]` and commits at most one explicit latched cancellation for that train on that tick. EB and power-off are independent latches: `pending_cancel_request` (with `cancellation_kind=EB` when EB writes it) and `pending_power_off` are written independently; same-tick acceptance drains EB first, then power-off. If both are accepted in the same tick, Phase 3 commits the EB cancellation through `pending_cancel_request` first; the later `pending_power_off` step finds no active path and proceeds to `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(...)` plus controller-local OFF cleanup without a second dispatcher cancel. Because v1 allows at most one active path per train, this payload does not need a required `path_id` routing key.
- `pending_route_abort` is a separate optional Phase-3 payload for explicit route-run teardown. Its canonical fields are `reason` and optional `expected_path_id` for diagnostics. API/UI/admin "abort route" requests write `pending_route_abort`; if an active path also exists, they also write `pending_cancel_request` with `cancellation_kind = PathCancellationKind.OPERATOR_CANCELLED` so Phase 3 cancels authority before removing the run. (System-driven non-operator aborts also use `OPERATOR_CANCELLED` for the paired cancel; v1 does not differentiate them in the cancellation enum because cancellation kind is diagnostic only and `pending_route_abort` is the actual teardown driver.) Route edit, retarget, reconciliation, EB, and ordinary path revocation that should continue the same `RouteRun` write only `pending_cancel_request` and must not set `pending_route_abort`.
- Phase-1-to-Phase-2 controller handoffs are also controller-owned, but they are not Phase-3 cleanup latches. V1 defines two such handoff flags: `departure_requested` and `dwell_entry_requested`. `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` sets these flags when it wants the controller to accept departure or enter dwell, then stops route planning for that train for the tick. `TrainController.tick(...)` consumes them after the operator-command drain and EB gate, before normal controller decisions. If a queued operator command in the same tick made the flag invalid (for example EB or power-off was accepted), the controller rejects or defers the handoff through `NotificationCenter`; Phase 1 does not call `TrainController.depart()` or `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()` directly.

**Phase-3 latched-cancel consumption when no active path**

- When Phase 3 reaches the `pending_cancel_request` consumption step and `PathDispatcher.active_path_id_by_train[train_id]` is already empty (because a movement-derived deviation cancellation already ran inline during queued-report drain, because the path completed normally earlier in the same Phase 3, or because no path ever existed for that train), the dispatcher cancel mutation step is skipped with a single diagnostic such as `latched cancel for <train_id> kind=<kind> skipped: no active path`. The latch is still consumed on that same Phase 3 pass. Phase 3 must not infer route-run removal from `cancellation_kind`; route-run teardown occurs only from `pending_route_abort`, `pending_power_off`, train removal, or terminal route completion.
- When Phase 3 reaches the `pending_route_abort` consumption step, it first assumes any active path has already been cancelled by the immediately preceding `pending_cancel_request` step or by an earlier movement-derived cancellation in the same pass. If `PathDispatcher.active_path_id_by_train[train_id]` is still set, this is a workflow violation: emit diagnostics, leave `pending_route_abort` set, and do not remove the `RouteRun`. Otherwise call `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(train_id, reason)` so the route assignment is fully removed, including dwell exit via `TrainController.finish_dwell()` if the controller was in `RUNNING_AT_STATION`, then clear `pending_route_abort`.
- `pending_power_off` is a separate Phase-3 latch, but power-off cleanup still contains the same dispatcher cancellation process as a substep. `TrainController.power_off_cmd()` is allowed while EB is latched if the train is stopped; EB release is not a prerequisite for shutdown. When Phase 3 reaches the `pending_power_off` consumption step, it first checks for a live active path. If one exists, it calls `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(active_path_id, cancellation_kind=PathCancellationKind.POWER_OFF, reason="power off")` and lets the ordinary cancellation callback run while the `RouteRun` is still registered. If no active path exists, only that dispatcher-cancel substep is skipped with a diagnostic. In both cases, Phase 3 then proceeds to `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(train_id, reason="power_off")` followed by the controller-local OFF cleanup (clear cab, EB latch, `eb_released`, `departure_confirmation_required`, `departure_ready_path_id`, `pending_power_off`, `ato_running`).
- Phase 3 must not call `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` on a `COMPLETED` or `CANCELLED` retained path row. That call would return failure with `"already completed"` / `"already cancelled"` and is unnecessary because the latch's intent has already been satisfied by the earlier inline mutation or by normal completion.

**Procedure / phase ownership table**

| Procedure | Detect / request in | Commit in | Cross-phase handoff |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| covered-waypoint normalization for fresh planning, target arming, `create_path(...)`, `extend_path(...)`, same-phase retry | Phase 1 | Phase 1 | none |
| in-flight covered-waypoint re-normalization within head-advance drain | Phase 3 | Phase 3 | none; uses already-armed `RouteRun.task_runs[current_waypoint_index].resolved_target` and current span coverage to consume consecutive boundaries on the same drain pass |
| explicit authority cancel / revoke request | API/UI/admin request handler or Phase 1 reconciliation code | Phase 3 | Set the targeted controller's `pending_cancel_request`; request handlers return accepted/pending once that payload is written and never call `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` inline. This latch cancels authority only and never removes a `RouteRun` by inspecting `cancellation_kind`. |
| explicit route abort request | API/UI/admin request handler | Phase 3 | Set the targeted controller's `pending_route_abort`, and also set `pending_cancel_request` when a live path exists. Phase 3 removes the `RouteRun` only from this abort latch after any active authority has already been cancelled or found absent. |
| EB activation (operator-issued via `activate_emergency_cmd()`, or ATC/no-authority escalation inside `TrainController.tick()`) | tick-entry command drain inside `TrainController.tick(...)` for the operator-issued path; later Phase 2 controller logic for the ATC/no-authority escalation path | Phase 3 | `pending_cancel_request` with `cancellation_kind=PathCancellationKind.EB` |
| usable-authority stop check | Phase 3 | Phase 3 | none; query post-physics controller state directly |
| dwell-complete timer check / `finish_dwell()` / terminal route removal | Phase 3 | Phase 3 | none; query route/controller dwell state directly |
| stopped-recovery cleanup (clear `RouteRun.path_cancelled` and/or `TrainController.eb_released`; reset `current_route_status.state` to `RUNNING`) | Phase 3 | Phase 3 | none; multi-tick latches gated by controller `IDLE` / `STANDBY`, `received_path_plan is None`, `eb_active == False`, `pending_power_off == False` |
| `power_off_cmd()` acceptance | Phase-2 operator-command drain | Phase 3 | `pending_power_off` |
| operator command acceptance (`power_on_cmd`, `set_cab`, `set_mode`, `set_manual_mode_type`, `set_handle`, `set_manual_power_percent`, `release_emergency_cmd`) | Phase-2 operator-command drain | Phase-2 operator-command drain | none |
| route/controller departure acceptance | Phase 1 route departure policy or explicit operator departure confirmation | Phase 2 | `departure_requested`; consumed by `TrainController.tick(...)`, which runs the `TrainController.depart()` acceptance logic |
| dwell-entry acceptance after dispatcher-recorded stop arrival | Phase 1 pending-dwell branch | Phase 2 | `dwell_entry_requested`; consumed by `TrainController.tick(...)`, which runs the `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()` acceptance logic |
| physics boundary / no-boundary movement observations | Phase 2 | Phase 3 | `queued_progress_reports` |
| deviation caused by movement | Phase 2 report creation | Phase 3 | `queued_progress_reports` |
| resource entry / tail-vacate release | Phase 2 report creation | Phase 3 | `queued_progress_reports` |
| dispatcher path completion | Phase 3 post-physics controller check | Phase 3 | none; `SimulationController` calls `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(...)` directly |

**Phase entity-chain table**

Each phase is a strict execution boundary within one train's per-tick transaction. Code must not cross into another phase's entity chain. Cross-phase communication uses only the latches defined in the Latch Table below.

| Phase | Entry point | Entity chain | Canonical writes | Must not |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Phase 1 — Setup** | `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(train_id, delta_seconds)` | `_advance_past_covered_waypoints(train_id)` → `_arm_next_target(train_id)` → `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` or `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` | `RouteRun.current_waypoint_index`, `RouteRun.task_runs[i].resolved_target`, `TrainController.departure_requested`, `TrainController.dwell_entry_requested`; `pending_cancel_request` when reconciliation demands a cancel | Call `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` directly; remove a `RouteRun`; clear controller authority; read Phase-2 or Phase-3 outputs |
| **Phase 2 — Execute** | `TrainController.tick(delta_seconds)` (operator-command drain first), then `TrainPhysics.tick(delta_seconds)` → `TrainController.on_physics_updated(event_time, span_update)` | Command drain in canonical order → pre-physics lookahead → ATC/ATO/manual command generation → physics integration → post-physics lookahead refresh | `TrainController` state fields, `eb_active`, `departure_ready_path_id`, `ato_running`, `queued_progress_reports`, `pending_cancel_request` (EB / ATC escalation), `pending_power_off` (`power_off_cmd()` acceptance) | Call `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` directly; mutate `RouteRun`; call `RouteExecutor` planning methods |
| **Phase 3 — Feedback** | `SimulationController` Phase 3 cleanup pass for that train | Drain `queued_progress_reports` via `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` → route callbacks via `RouteExecutor.handle_train_advanced(...)` → `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(...)` → dwell-complete check → stopped-recovery cleanup → `pending_cancel_request` consumption → `pending_route_abort` consumption → `pending_power_off` consumption → phase-end latch clear | Block/switch occupancy, `Path.status`, `Path.current_resource_index`, `RouteRun` progress cursors, `RouteTaskRun` arrival/departure times, controller authority (cleared via `receive_path_plan(None)`) | Create or extend authority; call `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` or `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)`; arm future targets |

**Latch table**

Rule: the last entity that uses a latch in the same 3-phase period is the one that clears it. Latches written during Phase 3 are not consumed in the same pass; they wait for the next tick's Phase 3. Phase-1-to-Phase-2 handoffs written during Phase 1 are consumed in the same tick's Phase 2; if a same-tick operator command invalidates the handoff, Phase 2 rejects and clears it via a `command_result` notification.

Inclusive-latch rule: when a higher-level latch is written, the same writer must also write the lower-level latches it implies. Inclusions: `pending_route_abort` includes `pending_cancel_request` (when a live path exists); `pending_power_off` includes `pending_cancel_request` (when a live path exists). EB activation writes `pending_cancel_request` only (no inclusion).

| Latch | Owner | Type | Written by (phase + entity) | Consumed by (phase + entity) | Gating state | Multi-tick |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `path_cancelled` | `RouteRun` | `bool` | Phase 3 — `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(...)` (invoked from `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` callback) | Phase 3 — stopped-recovery cleanup step | Controller `IDLE` or `STANDBY`; `received_path_plan is None`; `eb_active == False`; `pending_power_off == False` | Yes — survives across ticks until gating state is satisfied |
| `pending_cancel_request` | `TrainController` mailbox | `Optional[CancelPayload]` | Phase 1 (reconciliation), Phase 2 (command drain / ATC escalation), or API/UI handler (between ticks) | Phase 3 — explicit-cancel step; calls `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` when an active path exists; skips dispatcher call and consumes latch when no active path | None; always consumed on the first Phase 3 that reaches the step | No — last writer in same tick wins; always consumed in one Phase 3 pass |
| `pending_route_abort` | `TrainController` mailbox | `Optional[AbortPayload]` | API/UI/admin handler (same handler also writes `pending_cancel_request` when a live path exists — inclusive-latch rule) | Phase 3 — route-abort step; calls `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(...)` | `PathDispatcher.active_path_id_by_train[train_id]` must be empty at the abort step | Yes — deferred across ticks if active path still present at abort step |
| `pending_power_off` | `TrainController` mailbox | `bool` | Phase 2 — operator-command drain (`power_off_cmd()` acceptance; train must be stopped); same writer also writes `pending_cancel_request` when a live path exists (inclusive-latch rule) | Phase 3 — power-off step; runs after normal progress/completion/dwell/recovery has run in that same Phase 3 pass | None; consumed unconditionally once the Phase 3 pass reaches the step | No — consumed in the Phase 3 pass of the same tick |
| `eb_released` | `TrainController` | `bool` | Phase 2 — operator-command drain (`release_emergency_cmd()` acceptance) | Phase 3 — stopped-recovery cleanup step (same step that checks `path_cancelled`) | `received_path_plan is None`; controller `IDLE` or `STANDBY` | Yes — survives until gating state is satisfied |
| `departure_requested` | `TrainController` | `bool` | Phase 1 — `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` (route departure policy or ATO) | Phase 2 — `TrainController.tick(...)` handoff substep; calls `TrainController.depart()` | Live authority present; no same-tick operator command that invalidates it | No — consumed in Phase 2 of the same tick |
| `dwell_entry_requested` | `TrainController` | `bool` | Phase 1 — `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` (pending-dwell branch, after a stop completion is recorded) | Phase 2 — `TrainController.tick(...)` handoff substep; calls `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()` | Controller stopped in platform block; no same-tick invalidating operator command | No — consumed in Phase 2 of the same tick |
| `departure_confirmation_required` | `TrainController` | `bool` | Phase 2 — `release_emergency_cmd()` acceptance in command drain | Departure-confirmation API handler (between ticks) or Phase 1 departure policy | Explicit operator departure confirmation | Yes — cleared only when departure is confirmed |
| `departure_ready_path_id` | `TrainController` | `Optional[str]` | Phase 2 — `TrainController.depart()` acceptance | Phase 3 — usable-authority stop check (read-only reference); cleared when authority is cancelled or completed | Departure accepted | Yes — cleared when authority is cleared or a new path begins |
| `queued_progress_reports` | `TrainController` mailbox | `List[ControllerProgressReport]` | Phase 2 — `TrainController.on_physics_updated(event_time, span_update)` (one report per resource boundary, plus one no-boundary report per tick) | Phase 3 — progress-report drain; `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` for each, in FIFO order | None; always drained in full | No — cleared atomically during Phase 3 drain |

**`SimulationController.update()` calls**

- `self.route_executor.update(delta_seconds)` becomes a pure per-tick scheduling / Phase 1 preparation call. It advances route-executor bookkeeping time and prepares only the route-managed scheduling state that `SimulationController` consumes for this tick; it does not own the outer train loop and does not call `SimulationController._update_train(...)` itself.
- `SimulationController._update_train(train, delta_seconds)` remains the per-train Phase 2 execute step plus the immediate Phase 3 consume pass for that same train.
- `SimulationController` should stay the sole owner of executing the per-tick loop, while `RouteExecutor` owns the train ordering. `SimulationController.update(...)` advances time, calls `RouteExecutor.update(delta_seconds)` to refresh route-managed scheduling state, asks `RouteExecutor.order_runnable_trains(candidate_train_ids)` for the final ordered runnable-train list by effective priority (`Route.priority` for route-managed trains, default `5` for non-route/one-off trains; `9` highest, `0` lowest, stable order for ties), calls `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(train_id, delta_seconds)` as that train's complete Phase 1 route-executor tick immediately before that same train's `SimulationController._update_train(train, delta_seconds)` transaction, and then updates signals. The runnable list includes all powered trains plus powered-off trains that have queued operator commands. If a train is `OFF` at the start of its transaction, route Phase 1 is skipped for that transaction, but `TrainController.tick(...)` still runs its tick-entry command drain so `TrainController.power_on_cmd()` can be accepted. If the controller remains `OFF` after that command drain, skip route/controller decision work and skip Phase 3 for that transaction, but still run the Phase 2 `TrainPhysics.tick(...)` integration and `TrainController.on_physics_updated(...)` storage callback; powered-off trains do not skip physics. If the command drain turns power on, continue the normal powered Phase 2 controller/physics flow and run the normal Phase 3 feedback/cleanup pass in that same transaction; fresh route planning still waits for the next eligible Phase 1 tick. For trains with no current route-managed Phase 1 work, `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` is a no-op. `SimulationController` must not forward resource-entry or tail-vacate events itself.
- `SimulationController` should own one shared `PathDispatcher` instance for all trains. `TrainPhysics.tick(delta_seconds)` should compute the next span/speed plus tile delta and invoke `TrainController.on_physics_updated(event_time, span_update)` for that train on every tick, including ticks where the controller remains `OFF`. For powered transactions, `TrainController.on_physics_updated(event_time, span_update)` applies the update to controller-owned span/speed and enqueues ordered resource-entry, typed tail-vacate, and deviation observations into that train's Phase 3 mailbox; it does not call `PathDispatcher` directly. For a transaction where the controller remains `OFF`, the same callback still applies the controller-owned span/speed update, but it must not enqueue dispatcher/route Phase 3 progress reports because Phase 3 is skipped for that transaction. `TrainController.tick(...)` may also set `pending_power_off`, but it does not latch path completion. After Phase 2, `SimulationController` runs one Phase 3 consume pass for powered trains; for a controller that remains `OFF`, Phase 3 is skipped for that transaction even though Phase 2 physics ran. The Phase 3 drain consumes queued progress reports in order; that same drain is the only place movement-derived deviation is committed, and each route head-advance callback may consume at most the current next waypoint for that callback's reported block advance. If a tick crossed multiple boundaries, later queued callbacks in the same drain may then discover and consume later waypoints one callback at a time. After the drain, if an active path still exists for that train, `SimulationController` runs the post-physics usable-authority stop check against the controller's just-updated state and, on success, samples the current system Unix timestamp as `event_time` before calling `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(train_id, event_time)`. After that normal completion chance, Phase 3 runs the dwell-complete evaluation, then the stopped-recovery cleanup (clear `RouteRun.path_cancelled` and/or `TrainController.eb_released` when the controller is in `IDLE` / `STANDBY` with `received_path_plan is None`, EB and power-off latches clear), then consumes `pending_cancel_request`, `pending_route_abort`, and `pending_power_off` in that order. If the active path was already cleared earlier in the same Phase 3 pass, `pending_cancel_request` skips only the dispatcher cancel mutation and consumes the latch; `pending_route_abort` removes the `RouteRun` only from its own explicit latch; `pending_power_off` skips only its dispatcher-cancel substep and still continues route-run removal plus controller-local OFF cleanup. Path completion is therefore committed before explicit admin cancel/OFF cleanup, while movement-derived deviation cancellation still wins inline during queued progress consumption. `PathDispatcher` resolves active authority from its own registries while resolving post-authority physical release from the resource itself rather than by reading stale controller inbox state.
- `self.network.update_signals_from_blocks()` should run after dispatcher feedback so signals reflect all block-state changes, including path completion.
- Remove `self.path_dispatcher.get_and_clear_completion_events()` and the loop that calls `route_executor.handle_path_completion()`. Completion should be synchronous inside `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(train_id, event_time)` after Phase 3 has drained that train's post-move progress reports.

**SimulationController runtime surface**

- `SimulationController` is the registered runtime surface shared by `TrainController`, `PathDispatcher`, `RouteExecutor`, and `TrainPhysics` whenever any simulation runtime is active. It is the canonical home for runtime services and is the only owner of the public `add_train(...)` API surface. When a `SimulationController` instance exists for a network, it must finish initializing and registering this surface before any `TrainController` instance is constructed.
- `SimulationController` is optional in editor mode. Pure map-editor sessions (geometry placement, connectivity wiring, static field edits that do not affect runtime occupancy or locks) run with no `SimulationController`, no `BlockManager` runtime, no dispatcher, and no controllers; entities that hold a reference to `SimulationController` MUST type it as `Optional[SimulationController]` and tolerate `None`. An editor session that wants live track/switch testing MAY instantiate a lightweight `SimulationController` (with `BlockManager` wired but no runtime route/dispatcher trains) so that switch state changes route through the canonical mutation workflow normally. The choice is binary at the editor session level — there is no partial wiring.
- The v1 surface exposes exactly the shared services and registries needed by those components: `network`, `block_manager`, `path_dispatcher`, `route_executor`, `notification_center`, the controller registry keyed by `train_id`, the physics registry keyed by `train_id`, `get_train(train_id)`, `get_or_create_controller(train_id)`, `get_or_create_physics(train_id)`, route-event delivery methods such as `notify_path_completed(...)` / `notify_path_cancelled(...)`, a read-only route target projection such as `get_active_route_target(train_id)`, and the public train-addition API `add_train(...)`. `get_active_route_target(train_id)` returns the currently armed `RouteRun.task_runs[target_waypoint_index].resolved_target` when one exists and `None` otherwise; it is a read-only projection used for stop-versus-wait classification and must not rewrite route state. `add_train(...)` is owned by `SimulationController` because the train-addition API is invoked from the frontend/client layer to introduce a new train into the running simulation; it constructs the `Train`, ensures the controller exists, and writes the initial `TrainController.current_span` from placement input before any simulation tick. `add_train(...)` is the one accepted exception to the phase-only-mutation rule: it runs synchronously and is all-or-nothing. The implementation must serialize it against the per-tick phase loop so it executes only between simulation ticks; if a tick is in progress when the request arrives, the call must block until the tick has finished its post-tick signal update before the new train is registered. If any sub-step fails (validation, span derivation, controller registration), the partial state must be rolled back and the call must return failure rather than registering a partially wired train. A request whose `train_id` already exists must fail without mutating state.
- External operation surfaces exposed through routes/API/UI/admin code honor the same phase workflow as internal code. A request that wants to cancel/revoke a live path resolves the target train/controller, writes that controller's `pending_cancel_request` payload (`cancellation_kind`, optional `reason`, optional `expected_path_id`), and returns success once that accepted/pending payload has been written. A request that wants to abort the whole route run writes `pending_route_abort` and, when a live path exists, also writes `pending_cancel_request`; route-run removal is driven by that abort latch rather than by the cancellation enum. A request that sends an operator command returns success once the command has been queued. Any later semantic failure is emitted by the real command or dispatcher operation when the command queue or Phase-3 handoff is actually consumed, through `NotificationCenter`. The later per-train Phase 3 pass is the only place that calls `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)`, clears authority, releases future resources, removes route runs, and invokes route callbacks. Request handlers must not mutate dispatcher path records, controller authority, route-run state, block reservations, or switch locks directly.
- When a `SimulationController` instance is created for a runtime session, it is the first runtime object initialized for that network run. `SimulationController.__init__` constructs/registers `BlockManager`, `PathDispatcher`, and `RouteExecutor`, wires them and every already-loaded network entity (including each `Switch`) to the same `SimulationController` reference so that cross-entity callbacks such as `Switch` → `BlockManager.on_switch_state_changed(switch_id)` resolve through `self.simulation_controller.block_manager` rather than requiring direct injected back-references, and only then allows `SimulationController.get_or_create_controller(train_id)` to create a `TrainController`. Any later-created or later-loaded entity must be registered with this same `SimulationController` before it is allowed to mutate cross-entity runtime state. If that surface is not registered yet, `SimulationController.get_or_create_controller(...)` must fail rather than constructing a partially wired controller.
- Every `TrainController` stores only a reference to `SimulationController`, not separate dispatcher or route-executor references. Controller-owned release/removal commands do not call dispatcher or route teardown inline anymore; they only write the appropriate Phase 3 handoff (`pending_cancel_request`, `pending_route_abort`, `eb_released`, and/or `pending_power_off`). That same train's later Phase 3 consume pass then performs `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)`, any required `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(train_id, reason)` call, route stopped-recovery cleanup, and controller-local cleanup in canonical order.
- `PathDispatcher` and `RouteExecutor` also resolve controllers through `SimulationController.get_or_create_controller(train_id)` and must not keep private controller registries or construct controllers directly.

**Canonical runtime procedures**

These named procedures are the canonical home for ordering-sensitive cross-component workflows. Later sections may restate local consequences, but they should cross-reference these procedures rather than inventing a second order.

- **Physical progress / deviation procedure**:
  1. `TrainPhysics.tick(...)` emits one ordered `PhysicsSpanUpdate` per crossed resource boundary, plus one final no-boundary callback every tick so Phase 3 always has a deterministic consume point.
  2. `TrainController.on_physics_updated(event_time, span_update)` writes the new controller-owned span/speed, converts the tile delta into one typed `ControllerProgressReport`, and enqueues that report into the per-train Phase 3 mailbox. It does not call `PathDispatcher` directly.
  3. During that same train's Phase 3 consume pass, `SimulationController` drains `queued_progress_reports` in FIFO order by calling `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` for each queued report.
  4. `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` handles entered resources before any path bookkeeping. For each entered block it calls `BlockManager.occupy_blocks([block_id], train_id)`; for each entered switch it first compares the incoming `train_id` with the current `Switch.occupied_by`, and only on a clean entry does it set `Switch.occupied_by = train_id` and convert applicable same-path switch/flank locks into train-owned physical protection. Physical occupancy is the top-priority truth: if a train physically enters a resource and recording that entry would not overwrite another owner's live claim, the resource state must record it even if the software authority state is already wrong.
  5. If recording that physical entry would overwrite another train's current physical/reservation owner, `PathDispatcher` enters the canonical fatal physical-entry conflict procedure below immediately, leaves the prior owner intact, and stops processing the report.
  6. If `report.deviation == true`, dispatcher maps the structured controller deviation to a diagnostic cancellation kind after recording clean physical entries: `deviation_kind == "out_of_sequence"` becomes `PathCancellationKind.OUT_OF_SEQUENCE`, and `deviation_kind == "beyond_authorized_target"` becomes `PathCancellationKind.AUTHORITY_OVERSHOOT`. It does not run normal `Path.current_resource_index` advancement or `RouteExecutor.handle_train_advanced(...)` for that report. This movement-derived cancellation is committed immediately, inline during queued-report drain; it is not deferred to the later explicit-cancel latch. The canonical cancellation context is delivered upward only after the dispatcher cancel mutation has committed for that train on that same tick; Phase 3 route-owned cleanup clears live path correlation and stops there. It does not choose a fresh target, call `RouteExecutor._skip_waypoint(...)`, or create fresh authority while the train is still braking under the cancelled authority. The next eligible Phase 1 reruns ordinary covered-waypoint normalization plus the Phase-1 target-arm step from the train's then-current physical position; pass-through skip and stop retarget/completion behavior are decided by the general waypoint-kind rules, not by the cancellation kind. `OUT_OF_SEQUENCE` and `AUTHORITY_OVERSHOOT` therefore share the same recovery path.
  7. If `report.deviation == false`, dispatcher applies ordinary active-path head progress and route head-advance callbacks. For each entered block advance, after `Path.current_resource_index` is updated, the route side compares that single reported block/direction with the current next waypoint on the `RouteRun` directed route. If that waypoint is finished or passed, the callback advances route progress for that waypoint, clears any consumed armed target, resets `target_waypoint_index = current_waypoint_index`, and returns. It does not immediately persist or arm the following waypoint from the same callback. If the same physics tick crossed another boundary, the later queued progress callback is what calls pure next-waypoint lookup again and checks the next waypoint. The callback may read pure route-mode progression and membership/reverse-index data needed to test the current reported advance, but it must not call the Phase-1 target-arm step, must not write a new planning `resolved_targets` chain for a future leg, must not update `target_waypoint_index` as proof of authority planning for the next leg, and must not call `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` or `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)`. Fresh target arming and fresh authority creation/growth wait for the next eligible Phase 1.
  8. Dispatcher processes `tail_vacated_resources` after all entered-resource handling above.
- **Active-path completion procedure**:
  1. Stop-completion observation is post-physics in v1. `TrainController.tick(...)` maintains lookahead and stopped-only command handling, but it does not latch or emit path completion.
  2. `TrainPhysics.tick(...)` moves the train for that tick, and `TrainController.on_physics_updated(...)` writes the post-move span/speed plus any resource/deviation observations into the controller-owned Phase 3 mailbox.
  3. Later in that same train's Phase 3 consume pass, `SimulationController` drains queued progress reports first by calling `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` for each queued report.
  4. After queued progress has been fully consumed, including any movement-derived deviation handling from those reports, if an active dispatcher path still exists for that train, `SimulationController` asks the controller's post-move state whether the train is now stopped at the usable end of that live authority. Plain English: this means "the train has already moved for this tick and is now fully stopped at the controller's usable authority stop boundary." Explicit latched admin teardown such as `pending_cancel_request` and `pending_power_off` is processed only after this normal completion chance.
  5. The usable-authority stop check is true only when all of the following are true: the controller still has live authority, the current authority has been accepted for departure (`departure_ready_path_id == received_path_plan.path_id`), the selected cab/travel frame is heading in the active authority frame toward the current authority end, `TrainController.is_stopped()` is true, `TrainController.eb_active == False`, and the distance-0 lookahead has `atc_limit == 0` with `atc_limit_reason_code == "authority_stop"`. The train may satisfy this check either because it physically progressed through the final `resource_sequence` entry or because the remaining projected authority distance is too short to grant additional positive clearance after applying the controller safety margin. The check does not require physical head occupancy in the requested destination block, a route-authored centimeter stop target, or a second exact-position test.
  6. If that post-physics controller check is false, Phase 3 does not attempt path completion for that train on that tick.
  7. If the post-physics controller check is true, Phase 3 samples the current system Unix timestamp as `event_time` and calls `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(train_id, event_time)` directly rather than routing completion back through `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)`.
  8. `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(...)` accepts completion only when the active path still exists, `Path.authority_end_block_id == Path.requested_destination_block_id`, and the terminal authority block entry's block-relative traversal direction equals `Path.requested_destination_direction`. Dispatcher computes that terminal traversal direction from the final block `resource_sequence` entry's explicit directed frame and the block's canonical ordered tiles. Dispatcher does not require the train head to physically occupy the requested destination block; the controller safety margin / ATC stop boundary is the higher-priority stopping rule.
  9. On accepted completion, `PathDispatcher` follows the canonical terminal-teardown order: release not-yet-entered block reservations through `BlockManager.release_path_block_reservations(..., completed=True)`, release not-yet-entered switch/flank locks by scanning `Path.resource_sequence`, clear controller authority by calling `TrainController.receive_path_plan(None)`, call `TrainController.on_path_complete()` so the controller transitions to the state-machine-derived stopped ready state, transition `Path.status` to `COMPLETED`, clear `active_path_id_by_train[train_id]`, and then call `RouteExecutor.handle_path_completion(...)`.
  10. `RouteExecutor.handle_path_completion(...)` records the matching stop as a pending dwell step, records the arrival time for dwell, and clears the route-owned active-path correlation. The stop waypoint is not marked finished yet; it becomes finished only when the dwell step exits. The completion callback does not call `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()`.
  11. On the next eligible Phase 1 tick, a run with a recorded pending dwell writes `TrainController.dwell_entry_requested` before any new departure/path planning, then the following Phase 2 handoff consume slot enters `RUNNING_AT_STATION` if the controller preconditions still hold. While the run is pending dwell or dwelling, Phase 1 must not create or extend authority. The same train's later Phase 3 dwell-complete cleanup checks `RouteTask.should_depart(...)`; when the dwell timer is satisfied, it marks that dwell task finished, calls `TrainController.finish_dwell()`, and leaves the next target unarmed for a later Phase 1 tick.
- **Power-off procedure**:
  1. `TrainController.power_off_cmd()` is processed in the tick-entry **operator-command processing slot** inside `TrainController.tick(...)`. It validates only the stopped-only shutdown precondition for acceptance; it is valid while `eb_active == True` as long as the train is stopped. On acceptance it immediately disengages any active ATO run by setting `ato_running = False` and sets `TrainController.pending_power_off = True`. It does not cancel dispatcher authority, clear EB, or remove route state inline.
  2. The first eligible Phase 3 cleanup for that train consumes `pending_power_off` only after that train's queued progress and any eligible normal path completion have already been handled. If an active dispatcher path still exists at that point, `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(active_path_id, cancellation_kind=PathCancellationKind.POWER_OFF, reason="power off")` runs while the train's `RouteRun` is still registered, so the ordinary cancellation callback can clear route-owned correlation. If the active path was already cleared earlier in the same Phase 3 pass (movement-derived cancel, normal completion, or no path ever existed), this dispatcher cancel step is skipped per the **Phase-3 latched-cancel consumption when no active path** rule and Phase 3 proceeds straight to step 3.
  3. After the dispatcher mutation has committed and the ordinary cancellation callback path has run, that same Phase 3 cleanup calls `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(train_id, reason="power_off")` through `SimulationController.route_executor`.
  4. Finally that same Phase 3 cleanup performs controller-local OFF cleanup: transition to `OFF`, clear the active cab selection, clear any EB latch, clear `eb_released`, clear `departure_confirmation_required`, clear `departure_ready_path_id`, and set `pending_power_off = False`.
- **Fatal physical-entry conflict procedure**:
  1. `SimulationController` owns the unrecoverable failed-runtime flag and fatal diagnostic payload.
  2. `PathDispatcher` or `BlockManager` reports a typed `FatalPhysicalEntryConflict` to `SimulationController.enter_failed_runtime(...)` when a physical entry would overwrite another train's `occupied_by`, another train's `reserved_for_train`, another train's `Switch.occupied_by`, or enter a disabled block/switch. The payload is intentionally small and branchable: `resource_type`, `resource_id`, `incoming_train_id`, `prior_owner_train_id: Optional[str]`, `conflict_kind`, and active path IDs when known. `prior_owner_train_id` is populated for conflicting train ownership and is `None` for `conflict_kind == "disabled_resource_entry"`.
  3. `SimulationController.enter_failed_runtime(...)`, not the reporting entity, is the only writer of `SimulationController.failed_runtime = True`. It records the fatal payload, emits a `fatal_runtime_failure` notification through `NotificationCenter`, aborts the current simulation transaction immediately, and leaves all controller inboxes, path rows, route runs, block states, switch states, and physical occupancy exactly as they were at the moment of detection for inspection.
  4. Fatal conflict is not an ordinary cancellation/recovery branch. `SimulationController.enter_failed_runtime(...)` must not call `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)`, `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(...)`, or `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(...)`, because those APIs are recoverable workflow callbacks and would incorrectly clear state or replan while the runtime is failed.
  5. `SimulationController.update(...)` refuses further simulation advancement while the failed-runtime flag is set. V1 defines no automatic software resume. The UI/admin "release runtime failure" button is only an acknowledgement/resume gate after the operator has manually removed the problematic train or otherwise repaired the conflicting resource state. That release action calls a runtime-owned helper such as `SimulationController.release_failed_runtime_after_manual_cleanup()`, verifies that the fatal resource no longer has the conflicting physical owner / disabled-entry condition reported in the stored fatal payload, clears `failed_runtime` and the fatal payload, and emits a resume notification. It must not cancel paths, remove route runs, clear controller authority, or mutate resource ownership itself; those cleanup actions happen only through the explicit manual/admin repair workflow before the release button is accepted.

**Path completion notification flow**

- Remove `_complete_path_on_head_arrival()` and replace normal runtime completion with the canonical **Active-path completion procedure** above. This is not a rename: the trigger changes from head-arrival or head-tile-equals-last-path-tile to the post-physics controller check "the train is now fully stopped at the usable end of live authority", the notification chain changes from an event queue or synthetic stop-complete report to a direct Phase-3 call `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(train_id, event_time)`, and the method body changes accordingly.
- `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(train_id, event_time)` should commit completion only when the post-physics controller stop check has already passed for that train and the active path's authority end has reached the path's requested target block and direction. Completion ordering is exactly the **Active-path completion procedure**: release not-yet-entered resources, clear controller authority, call `TrainController.on_path_complete()`, transition the path to `COMPLETED`, clear `active_path_id_by_train[train_id]`, then call `RouteExecutor.handle_path_completion(...)` using the canonical completion payload from the **route path-event callback schema**. Completion release may drop not-yet-entered reservation ownership and may force-clear `CAUTION` for matching still-reserved blocks, but it must not force-clear any `OCCUPIED` resource the train still physically occupies.
- `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(train_id, event_time)` returns a `PathCompletionResult` with this exact shape:

  | Field | Type | Meaning |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  | `success` | `bool` | `True` only when dispatcher accepted and committed completion. `False` means no completion mutation was committed. |
  | `path_id` | `Optional[str]` | The active path ID that was completed or considered. `None` only when no active path existed for `train_id`. |
  | `new_status` | `Optional[PathStatus]` | `PathStatus.COMPLETED` on success; the unchanged current status such as `PathStatus.ACTIVE` on a failed predicate; `None` when no path row exists. |
  | `reason` | `Optional[str]` | `None` on success. Required diagnostic string on failure, such as `"no active path"`, `"active path missing"`, or `"authority end has not reached requested destination block/direction"`. |

  On `success=False`, `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(...)` leaves the path, controller authority, route state, and resources unchanged except for optional diagnostics. `SimulationController` may log or surface the failure result, but it must not synthesize a fallback completion or cancellation from that failure.
- `PathDispatcher`, `RouteExecutor`, and `TrainController` communicate with other runtime components through a `SimulationController`-owned runtime services interface rather than storing concrete cross-component back-references. The interface exposes controller lookup, route-event delivery, dispatcher mutation services, route-run target projection, and notifications as named methods. `PathDispatcher` calls the route-event methods on that interface, not a concrete `RouteExecutor` reference; `TrainController` writes latches and calls only controller/runtime-service methods, not route planning helpers directly.
- Wiring pattern: `SimulationController` is initialized first and is the canonical shared runtime owner.
