If the path request succeeds, the path record's `requested_destination_block_id`, `requested_destination_direction`, and `authority_end_block_id` become the dispatcher-owned stored target contract for that successful live segment, while `RouteRun.task_runs[i].resolved_target` retains the route-owned meaning of that segment for callbacks and deferred recovery. A later failed retry may prepare a different next-attempt station candidate, but it does not rewrite the dispatcher-owned stored contract of the already-successful live segment until a later successful create/extend overwrites it. Platform reselection is route-owned and occurs only after create/extend failure without authority creation or growth, cancellation, route-edit reconciliation, or explicit operator retargeting. For minimal v1 coverage, operator/admin edits to platform/block target-resolution inputs (for example platform block membership/order, target block disablement, or `Block.allowed_travel_directions` changes) do not rewrite an already-successful live segment; they take effect the next time Phase 1 arms a target before a new create/extend attempt.

**Canonical Next-Target Selection**

- `RouteExecutor._arm_next_target(train_id)` is the canonical Phase-1 target-arm step. It resolves the current shared `Route` from `exec_state.route_id`, reconciles `exec_state.route_version_seen` against `Route.route_version` using `Route.reconciliation_policy`, advances past covered tasks, resolves the concrete target for the current attempt, and writes `target_waypoint_index`, `direction`, `task_runs[i].resolved_target`, `current_route_status.state`, and `route_version_seen`. It internally computes the next-task index from `Route.tasks` and `Route.mode` (the read-only route-mode walk); that read-only index-advance logic is a private step inside `_arm_next_target` rather than a separately documented helper. Returning without writing a new `resolved_target` means there is no next task on this directed route walk and the current leg is terminal.
- In v1, `_arm_next_target` arms at most one task at a time; a station task's additional same-tick platform candidates are transient Phase-1 iteration state, not extra stored entries. When no next task remains on the reconciled directed route walk, `_arm_next_target` clears `task_runs[i].resolved_target`, sets `target_waypoint_index = current_waypoint_index`, sets `current_route_status.state = FINISHED`, and returns from planning for that train.
- If a next route-mode task exists but no legal concrete platform/block target can currently be resolved under the task's fixed/flexible platform policy, target arming fails before any dispatcher call. If no live authority exists and the current eligible Phase 1 exhausts every distinct same-tick station candidate, write `NO_PATH` through the `RouteRun.current_route_status` contract; if live authority exists, leave the live path and route status unchanged unless a later `extend_path(...)` result proves structural incompatibility.
- Callers must not independently rewrite `target_waypoint_index`, `direction`, or `task_runs[i].resolved_target` through raw numeric `+1` / `-1` index arithmetic or by reading `Route.tasks[target_waypoint_index]` outside `_arm_next_target`.
- `Route.mode` is consumed by the internal route-walk logic inside `RouteExecutor._arm_next_target(...)` only:
  - `RouteMode.ONE_OFF`: if no next waypoint exists on the current directed route walk, the current leg is terminal and ordinary route completion/unassignment follows the final completed stop or any explicit terminal skip that leaves no next waypoint. The train remains where it is in the state-machine-derived stopped ready state.
  - `RouteMode.CIRCULAR`: the helper wraps from the last waypoint to the first waypoint
  - `RouteMode.ROUND_TRIP`: the helper reflects at the route ends and returns the reversed traversal direction together with the next waypoint
- `RouteExecutor._arm_next_target(train_id)` is the canonical writer for leg arming, post-arrival advance, post-cancellation covered-task normalization, and route-edit reconciliation. Route-mode next-task selection is an internal private step inside it.
- The canonical `ROUND_TRIP` truth table for a three-waypoint route `[A, B, C]` is:
  - `(-1, forward) -> (0, forward)`
  - `(0, forward) -> (1, forward)`
  - `(1, forward) -> (2, forward)`
  - `(2, forward) -> (1, reverse)`
  - `(1, reverse) -> (0, reverse)`
  - `(0, reverse) -> (1, forward)`

**RouteTask data**

Retained current-code enums used by route data:

- `RouteMode`: `ONE_OFF`, `CIRCULAR`, `ROUND_TRIP`

`RouteTask` is the unified route-task schema in v1. It replaces the old `Waypoint` type. Every entry in `Route.tasks` is a `RouteTask`.

| Field | Type | Applies | Meaning |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `task_kind` | `"waypoint" \| "stop" \| "dwell"` | all | Required task behavior discriminator. `waypoint` is a pass-through traversal (no stop, no dwell). `stop` is a platform stop that enters dispatcher-committed completion. `dwell` is a hold at a location after a stop. |
| `task_location` | (one of `block_id`, `platform_id`, `station_id`) | all | Exactly one of these three must be present. `station_id`: logical station target resolved at runtime to a concrete platform. `platform_id`: fixed concrete platform target. `block_id`: direct concrete block target with no platform resolution. Supplying more than one, or none, is a route-definition validation error. |
| `task_time` | `Optional[float]` | all | Semantics depend on `task_kind`. For `waypoint`/`stop`: expected travel time in seconds from the previous task's location to this task's location; used to classify arrival as early/on-time/late when the `RouteTaskRun` timing data is available; omit when no timing classification is needed. For `dwell`: how long in seconds to remain at the location; omit to allow departure immediately (equivalent to `dwell_time_sec = 0` in the old model). |
| `task_direction` | `Optional["forward" \| "reverse" \| "either"]` | all | Route-level block-relative traversal preference. `RouteExecutor` resolves `"either"` to a concrete direction before any dispatcher call; the dispatcher never receives `"either"`. Defaults to `"either"` when omitted. |
| `flexible_platform` | `bool` | `waypoint`, `stop` (only for `platform_id` tasks) | Permits covered-task normalization to count any compatible concrete platform in the task's allowed set as satisfaction. Has no effect on same-tick retry, planning, or station-resolution. Defaults to `False`. Ignored for `block_id` and `station_id` tasks. |
| `target_time` | `Optional[float]` | `stop` | Optional absolute system-time Unix departure timestamp. When present and the train arrives late (`current_time >= target_time`), departure is granted immediately. |
| `departure_mode` | `Optional["auto" \| "manual"]` | `stop` | Optional departure override for this task; inherits `Route.default_departure_mode` when omitted. |
| `task_metadata` | `Optional[dict]` | varies | Small typed payload for kind-specific data, e.g. `flexible_platform` override for station/platform `waypoint`/`stop`, or a `departure_mode` override. Stored as a freeform dict; field names within are kind-scoped. |

Route-task authoring rules:

- A typical platform stop with a hold is authored as two consecutive tasks: `stop(task_location=platform_X, task_time=expected_travel_seconds)` followed by `dwell(task_location=platform_X, task_time=hold_seconds)`. The `stop` enters dispatcher-committed completion; the `dwell` holds the train in `RUNNING_AT_STATION` until the dwell timer elapses.
- A terminal stop with no hold is authored as a single `stop` task with no following `dwell` task (or with `task_time` omitted on the implied dwell). There is no `dwell_time_sec = 0` field equivalent; a missing `dwell` task is the correct encoding.
- A pass-through is a single `waypoint` task. It resolves to a concrete `ResolvedRouteTarget` for authority planning, is completed by head entry into the resolved block, and does not invoke dwell logic or `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()`.
- `task_kind` is required. Missing, null, or unrecognized `task_kind` is a validation error at task creation time; `RouteExecutor` must never infer a default kind.
- A terminal task must not be `waypoint`. If the train must stop and reverse immediately with no dwell, the terminal task is `stop`.
- For target resolution and path acquisition, `waypoint` and `stop` tasks use the same resolution chain and the same dispatcher target contract. Both resolve to a `ResolvedRouteTarget` for authority planning. The behavioral difference: a `stop` arrival is dispatcher-committed completion from the post-physics controller stop check, and the route's `RouteTaskRun` records `arrived_at`; a `waypoint` is completed by head entry with no stop, no dwell, and no `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()`.
- A `dwell` task does not create or require new dispatcher authority. While a `dwell` task is the current active task, Phase 1 does not create or extend authority. The `dwell` task is complete when its `task_time` has elapsed since `RouteTaskRun.arrived_at` (from the preceding `stop` task's `RouteTaskRun`), at which point Phase 3 dwell-complete cleanup marks the `dwell` task finished and leaves the next task unarmed for a later Phase 1.
- Station/platform distinction is explicit in the desired model:
  - `Station` is a logical grouping of platforms used by route planning and platform selection.
  - `Platform` is the concrete physical stop. When a waypoint resolves through station/platform data, dispatcher path requests, block IDs, and directions are always derived from one resolved concrete platform, never from a station group directly.
  - The current attempt may resolve one concrete `platform_id` when resolution goes through a platform. For a direct block-target waypoint, no platform resolution is needed.
- Define one canonical ordered block list for every concrete `Platform`, exposed as a public field such as `Platform.ordered_blocks`. This order is only the physical ordered extent of the platform, used to identify the two platform-end blocks and preserve edit stability. `Platform` carries no direction, no platform-local forward/reverse semantics, no per-block alignment mapping, and no user-facing directional option in v1. It is initialized directly from the block order supplied to backend platform-creation/edit logic. Backend validation must verify that the supplied block list is already one connected switch-free walk in that exact order; it must not reorder, reverse, or silently renormalize the caller-supplied sequence. This is a backend canonical-order contract, not a requirement to finalize a new public API shape in v1. `Platform.ordered_blocks` is preserved forever through runtime operation and changed only by block-edit operations that insert or remove linked blocks before or after the current sequence while preserving the surviving relative order, then reindex so the smallest platform-local index is `0`. An edit that would require a middle insertion, reorder, or reversal of the preserved physical order is invalid in v1 and must be rejected by backend edit validation. There is no legacy unordered-position migration fallback in v1: the refactor must update current platform creation/edit code so it supplies and preserves this canonical order directly.
- Remove platform-direction helpers from the target model. Do not add `Platform.block_direction_matches_platform`, `Platform.get_block_direction_from_platform()`, `required_platform_side`, or any equivalent platform-side direction contract. If a platform should serve only one direction, the user must configure the underlying end block's `Block.allowed_travel_directions` so only that traversal is allowed.
- `requested_destination_direction` is the resolved traversal constraint at the requested target. Task authors express the route-level preference as `forward`, `reverse`, or `either` on `RouteTask.task_direction`, but those values are block-relative preferences, not platform-relative preferences. By the time `RouteExecutor` builds `ResolvedRouteTarget`, it must have resolved that preference into one concrete block-relative `"forward"` or `"reverse"` for the chosen `requested_destination_block_id`. For a task that resolves through a concrete platform, the candidate comparison defined in the platform resolution subsection below evaluates terminal traversals through the platform end block(s); `task_direction == "forward"` or `"reverse"` filters out candidates whose terminal traversal does not match that authored block-relative direction or is not allowed by `Block.allowed_travel_directions`. `task_direction == "either"` applies no route-authored direction filter, but every candidate must still satisfy `Block.allowed_travel_directions`, and `RouteExecutor` still stores the chosen block-relative concrete direction. When a task targets a direct block, `"forward"` / `"reverse"` are interpreted directly in that block's immutable internal traversal order, and `"either"` means `RouteExecutor` picks whichever block-relative direction is reachable under the same current-span comparison and allowed by `Block.allowed_travel_directions`. Dispatcher never receives `"either"` in the v1 path contract; route resolution must always eliminate it before calling `create_path(...)` or `extend_path(...)`.
- Define the exact dispatcher terminal frame separately. The terminal frame is the explicit directed geometry used for controller braking and diagnostics: `path_tiles`, `entry_edge`, and `exit_edge` for the relevant terminal block. When a live path exists, dispatcher derives that frame from the chosen terminal `resource_sequence` block entry. Boundary/safety-margin projection must never infer that frame from `requested_destination_direction` alone.
- Resolve waypoint targets through one canonical chain: `station -> platform -> block`. If the waypoint already specifies a concrete `platform_id`, resolution starts at `platform`. If it specifies a direct `block_id`, resolution skips station/platform lookup and targets that block directly.
- When a route/waypoint targets a logical station rather than a fixed platform, `RouteExecutor` must resolve one concrete platform before path creation. In v1, platform availability is evaluated relative to the train for this route attempt. A platform is unavailable when any linked block is held by another train's physical occupancy or another train/path's live reservation. `CAUTION` by itself does not make a platform unavailable: a caution block may still be reserved/acquired when ordinary reservation rules allow it, and the caution state is consumed by `TrainController` lookahead as a lower ATC speed cap. The current train's own physical occupancy or current live authority must not make that platform unavailable to itself; same-train coverage is handled by covered-waypoint normalization and same-train path conflicts are handled by the shared pathfinder/acquisition rules. Deterministic tie-breaking is required and follows the canonical candidate-order rule below.
- Platform/station target selection in v1 is:
  - if the waypoint names a concrete `platform_id`, resolve exactly that platform for this tick. Pick its single best compatible platform-end candidate by the canonical graph comparison. If that create/extend attempt fails without creating or growing authority, wait until the next eligible tick rather than trying another platform or another platform end in the same Phase 1 pass.
  - if the waypoint names a logical `station_id`, build the complete ordered transient candidate list of all compatible available platform-end candidates across every platform in `Station.platform_ids`, sorted by the canonical graph comparison (`tile_count`, then `walk_length_cm`, then `candidate_rank`, then final `end_a` dead-heat only across current-span end choices). In that same Phase 1 pass, `RouteExecutor` may try each distinct resolved `(requested_destination_block_id, requested_destination_direction)` pair from that station candidate list at most once, stopping as soon as one create/extend attempt creates or grows authority.
  - if the waypoint names a direct `block_id`, resolve exactly that block/direction for this tick. If the create/extend attempt fails without creating or growing authority, wait until the next eligible tick rather than inventing alternatives.
- `flexible_platform` is not a same-tick retry switch for a concrete `platform_id` waypoint in v1. Because every waypoint must provide exactly one targeting field, a waypoint that wants "try any platform in this station" should target `station_id`; a waypoint that targets `platform_id` targets that one concrete platform.
- Station candidate enumeration preserves the route-specified `requested_direction` literally as a block-relative traversal preference. If the waypoint specified `forward` or `reverse`, candidate platform ends are compatible only when their terminal traversal satisfies that direction under the candidate block's `Block.allowed_travel_directions`. If the waypoint specified `either` (or omitted the field, which is treated as `either`), any `Block.allowed_travel_directions`-compatible terminal traversal may be used.
- Later retries follow the same route-side selection policy. A concrete platform retry keeps the same platform and recomputes its current best compatible end on the later tick. A station retry may re-enumerate all compatible available platform-end candidates for that station on the later tick. If no concrete platform/block candidate can be resolved for the retry under that policy, `RouteExecutor` fails target resolution for that retry and must not treat it as an `extend_path(...)`-side retarget problem.
- For covered normalization and subsequent task service on the current directed route walk, a station/platform task with `task_kind == "waypoint"` counts as satisfied when the train covers any compatible concrete platform in that task's allowed station/platform set; a station/platform task with `task_kind == "stop"` counts as satisfied only after the stop has been served by dispatcher-committed stop completion and dwell exit. When either satisfaction rule applies, `RouteExecutor` uses the actually covered or served compatible block/platform only to decide satisfaction and to report diagnostics; it must not force resolution to some other platform, must not write a persistent served-platform choice for later route-control decisions, and must not depend on a persistent per-task cache to remember that choice later.
- For each concrete platform being considered, `RouteExecutor` must gather the terminal candidate block at each end of that platform by calling the same shared network-graph walk query that `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` uses. For a multi-block platform, the candidate end blocks are the first and last entries of `Platform.ordered_blocks`; for a single-block platform, both platform ends collapse to the same block, so only that one block participates. If a cab is selected, only the current head-side start locator participates in this stop-planning comparison. If no cab is selected, both physical train ends may participate, and a switch-side end is first mapped inward to the occupied block at that end of the current span; if no such inward occupied block exists, that end is dropped from the comparison set. Each candidate graph-query result carries the terminal block-relative direction derived from the canonical block-direction helper through `Block.ordered_tiles`; that direction must pass the task direction filter and `Block.allowed_travel_directions`. For a concrete `platform_id` task, pick the shortest successful candidate using the canonical deterministic order from the shared graph query: lower `tile_count`, then lower `walk_length_cm`, then lower `candidate_rank`, with `end_a` used only as the final deterministic dead-heat tie-break if those metrics still remain exactly tied across the remaining current-span end choices. For a logical `station_id` task, keep the full sorted list of compatible successful platform-end candidates as transient same-Phase-1 retry state, and try each distinct resolved `(requested_destination_block_id, requested_destination_direction)` pair at most once in that pass. The chosen or currently attempted block becomes `requested_destination_block_id`, and the chosen block-relative direction becomes `requested_destination_direction`. This is how task-authored `"either"` is converted into one concrete `"forward"` or `"reverse"` for station/platform tasks. If no candidate succeeds, fail the task attempt. The chosen/current candidate block is the dispatcher-facing target block for both `stop` and `waypoint` tasks that resolve through station/platform data.
- For a task-authored `task_direction == "forward"` or `"reverse"`, the same candidate comparison is used, but pairs whose resulting block-relative direction does not match the authored direction are dropped from the comparison before selection. For `task_direction == "either"`, no route-authored direction filter is applied and the shortest allowed candidate wins.
- After that resolution, `RouteExecutor` derives the per-attempt `ResolvedRouteTarget` (`requested_destination_block_id`, block-relative `requested_destination_direction`) for the current task and passes only that transient route-owned request contract into `PathDispatcher`. There is no platform-frame direction to convert. For station targets with multiple same-tick candidates, `RouteExecutor` writes only the currently attempted candidate into `RouteRun.task_runs[target_waypoint_index].resolved_target` before each create/extend call; candidates not yet attempted remain transient Phase-1 iteration state. Callback-time code reads the route-owned `RouteRun.task_runs` state rather than performing fresh target resolution. Later planning retries enter `_arm_next_target` again only when the previous `create_path(...)` or `extend_path(...)` attempt did not create or grow authority, or when cancellation, route-edit reconciliation, or explicit retargeting cleared the prior attempt.
- In v1, every task must provide exactly one concrete targeting field among `block_id`, `platform_id`, and `station_id`. Supplying more than one is a route-definition error; supplying none is also a route-definition error. No extra type-discriminator field is needed because the mutually exclusive field set is itself the discriminator.
- A task with `task_kind == "stop"` may therefore target either:
  - a fixed concrete `platform_id`, or
  - a logical `station_id`, in which case `RouteExecutor` resolves a concrete `platform_id` for the current attempt before path creation
  - a direct concrete `block_id`, in which case no platform resolution is required
  If more than one of `block_id`, `platform_id`, or `station_id` is present, the route definition is invalid. If none of them is present for a stop task, the route definition is invalid.
- Keep platform targeting, dwell/timing rules, platform-selection flexibility, and block-relative direction policy.
- Remove platform-side direction constraints from the target model in v1. Compatibility depends only on block-level `Block.allowed_travel_directions` plus current availability; there is no separate `required_platform_side`, platform-direction field, platform-side helper, or fallback rule.
- Add explicit stop-policy data used by route arrival and same-block repositioning for tasks with `task_kind == "stop"`:
  - the resolved destination block for the task attempt
  - the block-relative traversal direction for that attempt
- Add an explicit route-owned traversal-constraint field, `requested_destination_direction`, on the per-attempt `ResolvedRouteTarget` for every task. Same-block / same-tile repositioning must not guess direction from geometry alone; before path creation, `RouteExecutor` must resolve the task-authored `task_direction` into one concrete block-relative `"forward"` or `"reverse"` for `requested_destination_block_id` and store that value on `ResolvedRouteTarget`. Dispatcher still derives the exact terminal frame from explicit terminal geometry, but it does so after route resolution has already eliminated `"either"` from the request contract.
- If the task targets a logical `station_id` or concrete `platform_id`, `requested_destination_block_id` is resolved only after `RouteExecutor` has picked a concrete platform for that train's current attempt and then applied the canonical shorter-path platform-end selection rule above. If the task targets a direct `block_id`, that block becomes `requested_destination_block_id` directly, but a disabled block is not a valid direct target. In all cases, the stored `requested_destination_direction` is the block-relative direction for that resolved block; there is no raw platform-level direction token in the route definition or resolved runtime state.
- Add optional `departure_mode` as a per-task override of `Route.default_departure_mode`. When unset, the task inherits the route default.
- Do not define a separate stop-window tolerance or route-authored stop-position field in v1. The controller safety margin is used by controller lookahead and ATO braking to shape the train's stop, but route completion does not manually verify a centimeter tolerance around any route value.
- For tasks with `task_kind == "stop"`, task arrival is not simply "the train is in the task block", but it is also not gated by an exact-stop tolerance check in v1. A stop task enters dwell only after dispatcher completion caused by the post-physics controller stop check for the active authority that reaches the resolved target, and that stop task is marked finished only when the following dwell task exits (or immediately if no dwell task follows). `task_kind == "waypoint"` is reached by train-head entry / physical pass of its resolved block. `PathCancellationKind.OUT_OF_SEQUENCE` and `PathCancellationKind.AUTHORITY_OVERSHOOT` cover the two controller deviation families.
- Add one shared boundary projection helper such as `RailwayNetwork.project_authority_boundary_delta(head_tile, head_entry_edge, head_distance_cm, authority_end_block_id, terminal_frame, safety_margin_cm) -> delta_cm`. Here `terminal_frame` is the exact directed terminal frame (`path_tiles`, `entry_edge`, `exit_edge`) for the authority-end block. It uses tile lengths and traversal geometry to project the live train head and the safety-margin-adjusted authority boundary onto the same forward axis, then returns their signed separation in centimeters on that shared axis: positive means the train head is past the safety-margin boundary in the forward axis, negative means it is still short of it, and zero is on-target. In v1 this helper is for braking/diagnostic projection only; it is not the authoritative path-completion gate, and `RouteExecutor` must not compare live train position against it itself.

**Task time semantics**

- Remove `TimingMode` entirely in v1. All stored route/task timestamps use system-time Unix seconds.
- `RouteTask.target_time`, when present on a `stop` task, is the absolute system-time Unix departure timestamp. If the train arrives late (`current_time_sec >= target_time` at dwell entry), `RouteTask.should_depart(...)` returns `True` immediately with no extra wait.
- `RouteTask.should_depart(arrived_at_time_sec, current_time_sec)` uses only system-time Unix seconds in v1:
  - if `target_time` is present, depart when `current_time_sec >= target_time`
  - otherwise, for a `dwell` task, depart when `current_time_sec - arrived_at_time_sec >= task_time` (where `task_time` is the dwell task's hold duration)
  - if neither `target_time` nor `task_time` is set on the dwell task, depart immediately
- A `stop` task with no following `dwell` task enters the ordinary stop-completion/dwell path but `should_depart(...)` returns `True` immediately so the route advances without waiting.

**Route-definition validation**

- A valid route definition must contain at least one `task_kind == "stop"` task in total. Single-stop routes are valid (e.g. one-off reposition or single-destination delivery); the single stop is both the first and final stop.
- Consecutive stop tasks that resolve to the same concrete target are valid. Route validation must not reject or collapse them merely because their resolved `requested_destination_block_id` / `requested_destination_direction` pair is identical; runtime stop handling decides whether the next stop is completed through the ordinary stop-completion/dwell path or used as an explicit local reposition destination. A stop task is not skipped solely because its block is already covered.
- Every task must provide `task_kind` explicitly as `"waypoint"`, `"stop"`, or `"dwell"`. Missing `task_kind`, null `task_kind`, or any other value is a validation error at task creation/update time.
- A route made only of `waypoint` tasks is invalid.
- The stored `Route.tasks` list must end in `task_kind == "stop"` (or a `dwell` task immediately following a `stop`) before a `RouteRun` may be created. The schedule editor/API must validate that terminal stop requirement before sending the route to route-run activation. `RouteMode.CIRCULAR` changes how the stored task list is interpreted during execution, but it does not remove the requirement that the authored list itself ends at a stop or dwell. The terminal task sequence is part of the route: the route is not complete until any terminal `dwell` task's timer has elapsed, or the terminal `stop` task has completed when no `dwell` follows it. A terminal `stop` with no following `dwell` is the canonical "stop and end immediately" shape; a terminal `stop` followed by a `dwell` with `task_time > 0` or `target_time` is the canonical "end after holding at the platform" shape. Both are valid.
- A bulb loop or other turnaround that returns to the same stop must be modeled explicitly in the task list (for example `A(stop) -> bulb(waypoint) -> dwell(A) -> A(stop)`). `RouteMode.ROUND_TRIP` does not infer that geometry from one terminal stop.
- Every `task_kind == "stop"` task must identify a valid stop target: either a concrete `block_id`, a concrete `platform_id`, or a logical `station_id` that can be resolved to a platform at runtime.
- `RouteExecutor` should reject or refuse to activate any route definition that violates these rules rather than trying to infer missing stop semantics at runtime.

**Route-run state**

This is the canonical field set for `RouteRun` in v1. `RouteExecutor.execution_states[train_id]` is a `Dict[str, RouteRun]` that stores at most one `RouteRun` for each train, with exactly the fields below. A single `Route` can still have many `RouteRun` instances because many trains may be assigned to the same shared route definition. Throughout the rest of this section the name `exec_state` is used as a short alias for the `RouteRun` belonging to the current `train_id` (i.e. `RouteExecutor.execution_states[train_id]`).

| Field | Type | Meaning |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `run_number` | `int` | Composite run-instance number assigned by `RouteExecutor` when this `RouteRun` is created. `RouteExecutor` keeps a stable ordered route registry as an instance field `RouteExecutor.route_registry: Dict[str, RouteRegistryEntry]` keyed by `route_id`, where each `RouteRegistryEntry` stores `route_registry_index: int` (append-only, assigned in insertion order starting at `0`, never recycled or compacted on route removal) and `run_count_for_route: int` (1-based count of runs created for that `route_id`). `COPY_COUNT_BASE = 1000000` in v1, and `run_number = route_registry_index * COPY_COUNT_BASE + run_count_for_route`. The per-route counter increments on every new run for that `route_id`. A route that is removed and later re-added receives a new `route_registry_index`. |
| `train_id` | `str` | The train this run belongs to. Also the key used in `RouteExecutor.execution_states`. |
| `route_id` | `str` | The shared `Route` definition this run is executing. The run itself is keyed by `train_id` because one train can execute only one route task at a time in v1. |
| `route_version_seen` | `int` | Latest `Route.route_version` whose waypoint order and execution policy have been reconciled into this run's cursor and target state |
| `started_at` | `Optional[float]` | System-time Unix timestamp when this run instance started. |
| `current_waypoint_index` | `int` | Physical-progress cursor: index in the current reconciled shared `Route.tasks` list of the last task the train has actually finished (waypoint-consumed, dwell-completed stop, or explicitly skipped). A dispatcher-completed stop arrival does not advance this cursor until the dwell task (when present) exits. **All route runs initialize to `-1` before the first completed task**, including one-off operator/local routes. `-1` is a sentinel, never a Python negative index. The first planning target is therefore always `Route.tasks[0]`. |
| `target_waypoint_index` | `int` | Planning cursor: index in the current reconciled shared `Route.tasks` list of the task the current live planning chain is presently targeting for the next point-to-point create/extend step on the current directed route walk. May be numerically greater than `current_waypoint_index` on forward legs or numerically smaller on reverse-round-trip legs. Equal to `current_waypoint_index` when no next task on the current directed route walk is armed. |
| `direction` | `"forward" \| "reverse"` | Effective traversal direction for the current directed route walk. Initialized to `"forward"`; reversible round-trip behavior comes from this field plus `RouteExecutor._find_next_task_target(...)`. All route-walk code reads and writes this field as the literal `"forward"` / `"reverse"` string. |
| `task_runs` | `List[RouteTaskRun]` | Per-run task table. One `RouteTaskRun` entry per `Route.tasks` index, parallel to the shared task list. Owns per-run arrival/departure timing, resolved target, and per-task error context — see **RouteTaskRun schema** below. Eliminates the old parallel `arrived_at_platform_time`, `dwell_target`, `last_waypoint_platform_id`, and `RouteRunResolvedTarget` fields. |
| `waiting_for_path` | `bool` | Pre-departure create-path failure indicator. User-visible explanation for why the train has no live authority: set when pre-departure platform lookup, current-block lookup, or dispatcher `create_path(...)` fails before any authority exists; cleared on successful path creation, authoritative path completion, and on `handle_path_cancellation(...)` for this run's current live path. Diagnostic only — Phase 1 / Phase 3 logic must read controller state (`received_path_plan is None`) for runtime branching, not this field. It must not be used for a live best-effort truncated path. |
| `last_error` | `Optional[str]` | Most recent route/path error text for this train's run (per-train; `Route` does not carry a shared last-writer-wins error summary in v1). |
| `error_time` | `Optional[float]` | System-time Unix timestamp of `last_error`. |
| `path_cancelled` | `bool` | Latch: set by `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(...)` when the matching live path is cancelled; consumed by Phase-3 stopped-recovery cleanup when the controller is `IDLE`/`STANDBY`, `received_path_plan is None`, `eb_active == False`, and `pending_power_off == False`. See the Latch Table in the Core Runtime Loop section for the full lifetime rule. |
| `current_route_status` | `RouteRunStatus` | Always-present route-owned status. Allocated with `state = "RUNNING"`, `resolved_targets = []`, `last_cancellation_kind = None`. `state` is user-visible diagnostic only and never gates runtime branching; control flow keys on `received_path_plan`, the `path_cancelled` latch (see Latch Table), controller cleanup latches, and current span coverage. See the write-contract table below for writers and triggers. |

- Operator/manual same-block or same-tile reposition is modeled through the ordinary one-off `Route` + `RouteRun` flow, with no special run-level flag. Such a route is created with `RouteMode.ONE_OFF` and follows the ordinary route-definition validation rules below. Because `current_waypoint_index` initializes to `-1` for every run, a one-off reposition can be authored as a single-task route whose only `task_kind == "stop"` task is the destination; `_arm_next_target` resolves to that stop as the first executable movement task. The general Phase 1 / `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` flow handles the local authority through the same-block synthetic-path branch already defined in the `create_path()` flow.

This is the canonical field set for `RouteRun.current_route_status` in v1.

| Field | Type | Meaning |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `state` | `"RUNNING" \| "NO_PATH" \| "DEVIATED" \| "FINISHED"` | Diagnostic only; never a branching gate. `RUNNING`: ordinary execution (incl. dwell and unarmed-between-legs). `NO_PATH`: last Phase 1 exhausted all same-tick targets without creating/growing authority while no live path exists (legal only when `received_path_plan is None`); next Phase 1 clears it back to `RUNNING` before retrying. `DEVIATED`: cancellation-backed recovery pending; written by `handle_path_cancellation(...)` only, never by completion mismatch. `FINISHED`: terminal walk complete, used mainly for one-off runs before removal. |
| `last_cancellation_kind` | `Optional[PathCancellationKind]` | Last cancellation enum recorded by `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(...)`, kept strictly for UI / log diagnostics. v1 has no functional consumer for this field; planning and routing decisions never read it. Recovery is unified across all kinds: Phase 1 is gated by controller state (`IDLE` / `STANDBY`), latches (`eb_active`, `pending_power_off`), `RouteRun` presence, `RouteRun.path_cancelled`, and current span coverage; waypoint-task skip and stop retarget/completion behavior are decided by the normal task-kind rules, not by this enum. |

`RouteRun.current_route_status` has this v1 write contract:

| Writer | Trigger | State after write | Other required writes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `_arm_next_target(train_id)` | a run adopts a fresh next target during normal planning or reconciliation | `RUNNING` | write `target_waypoint_index`, `direction`, `task_runs[target_waypoint_index].resolved_target`, and `route_version_seen`. Always overwrites any stale `DEVIATED` marker to `RUNNING` regardless of prior status; no separate reset step is required. |
| `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(...)` | the matching live path is cancelled or revoked | `DEVIATED` | clear the interrupted `task_runs[target_waypoint_index].resolved_target`, reset `target_waypoint_index = current_waypoint_index`, set `RouteRun.path_cancelled = True` (see Latch Table), and record `last_cancellation_kind` for diagnostics |
| `RouteExecutor.handle_path_completion(...)` mismatch branch | completion callback does not match the currently armed stop target (recoverable diagnostic, not fatal) | `RUNNING` (left unchanged) | clear `task_runs[target_waypoint_index].resolved_target`, leave `current_waypoint_index` unchanged, set `target_waypoint_index = current_waypoint_index`, record diagnostics on `last_error`/`error_time`, emit a route-mismatch notification through `NotificationCenter`. Do not write `DEVIATED`, do not call `SimulationController.enter_failed_runtime(...)`; the train is already stopped at the dispatcher-completed boundary and the next eligible Phase 1 plans normally from current span coverage. |
| planning kernel same-tick candidate exhaustion | the current eligible Phase 1 exhausts all distinct same-tick resolved targets without creating or growing authority while no live path exists | `NO_PATH` | clear `task_runs[target_waypoint_index].resolved_target`, set `target_waypoint_index = current_waypoint_index`, and stop Phase 1 for that train for that tick |
| Phase-3 stopped-recovery cleanup | `RouteRun.path_cancelled == True` and the controller has reached the stopped recovery state (`IDLE` or `STANDBY`, with EB and power-off latches clear) | `RUNNING` | clear `path_cancelled`, leave the next leg unarmed, keep `target_waypoint_index = current_waypoint_index`, and let a later eligible Phase 1 run ordinary covered-waypoint normalization plus the Phase-1 target-arm step |
| next eligible Phase 1 after `NO_PATH` | the run is again eligible to retry the same planning problem on a later tick | `RUNNING` | clear the stale `NO_PATH` marker, leave the next leg unarmed, and retry ordinary planning from current physical progress |
| `_arm_next_target(train_id)` | reroute planning succeeds after deviation, route edit, or explicit retargeting | `RUNNING` | rewrite `task_runs[target_waypoint_index].resolved_target` and update `route_version_seen` |
| `_arm_next_target(train_id)` or terminal completion/skip path | no next target remains on the current directed route walk | `FINISHED` | clear `task_runs[target_waypoint_index].resolved_target` and set `target_waypoint_index = current_waypoint_index` |

`RouteExecutor` owns these writes. Other entities such as `BlockManager`, `PathDispatcher`, and switch/resource callbacks may trigger the route callback that causes a state change, but they do not write `RouteRun.current_route_status.state` directly. In v1, `OPERATOR_CANCELLED`, `REVOKED`, `EB`, `OUT_OF_SEQUENCE`, `AUTHORITY_OVERSHOOT`, and `POWER_OFF` all write the same diagnostic `DEVIATED` state for the matching live leg before later recovery or removal. The functional gate is the `path_cancelled` latch (see Latch Table), not `current_route_status.state` or `last_cancellation_kind`. The `DEVIATED` marker is reset to `RUNNING` by the same train's Phase 3 stopped-recovery cleanup when that latch is set and the controller has reached `IDLE` or `STANDBY`; that cleanup does not arm a target or create authority. When a later tick retries after `NO_PATH`, the status returns to `RUNNING`; when the run is complete, it becomes `FINISHED` until ordinary route-run removal clears the object.

**RouteTaskRun schema**

`RouteTaskRun` is the per-run task-state entry. `RouteRun.task_runs` is a `List[RouteTaskRun]` parallel to `Route.tasks` — one entry per task index, allocated when the `RouteRun` is created and indexed by the same integer index as `Route.tasks`. `RouteTaskRun` is the canonical owner of arrival timing, resolved dispatcher target, and per-task error context. It replaces all former parallel fields (`arrived_at_platform_time`, `dwell_target`, `last_waypoint_platform_id`, and `RouteRunResolvedTarget`).

| Field | Type | Meaning |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `task_index` | `int` | Index into `Route.tasks`; matches the position of this entry in `RouteRun.task_runs`. |
| `resolved_target` | `Optional[ResolvedRouteTarget]` | Block and direction resolved by the Phase-1 target-arm step for this task's current attempt. Populated by `_arm_next_target(train_id)` when it arms this task; cleared when the dispatcher path for this task is cleared and `current_waypoint_index` advances past this task. `None` while the task has not yet been armed or after it has completed. |
| `arrived_at` | `Optional[float]` | System-time Unix seconds when the train arrived at this task's location. Set for `stop` tasks on dispatcher-committed stop arrival; set for `waypoint` tasks on head-entry; set for `dwell` tasks on dwell-entry. `None` until the task is active and arrival is recorded. |
| `finished_at` | `Optional[float]` | System-time Unix seconds when this task was finished. For `dwell` tasks, this is dwell-exit time. For `stop` tasks followed by a `dwell`, `finished_at` is set when the `dwell` exits. For `stop` tasks with no following `dwell`, `finished_at` equals `arrived_at`. For `waypoint` tasks, `finished_at` equals `arrived_at`. |
| `delay_class` | `Optional["early" \| "on_time" \| "late"]` | Timing classification computed when `arrived_at` is recorded, by comparing `task_time` (from `Route.tasks[task_index]`) with the elapsed time from the previous task's `finished_at`. `None` when `task_time` is absent on the shared task, when the task has not yet been reached, or when no preceding task exists. |
| `last_error` | `Optional[str]` | Most recent per-task error text recorded during a failed planning or completion attempt for this task. Diagnostic only. |
| `error_time` | `Optional[float]` | System-time Unix timestamp of `last_error`. |

`ResolvedRouteTarget` is the resolved dispatcher request contract for one task attempt: `requested_destination_block_id: str` and `requested_destination_direction: "forward" | "reverse"`. It supersedes `ResolvedWaypointTarget`.

Phase-1 target-arm step is the only writer of `task_runs[i].resolved_target`. The resolved target lives for the lifetime of one live path attempt for its task: built when `_arm_next_target(train_id)` resolves the current task and path creation begins, cleared when the dispatcher path for that task is cleared and `current_waypoint_index` advances past the task. After that, the next task is identified directly through `Route.tasks[current_waypoint_index + offset]` and `_arm_next_target(...)` resolves a fresh `ResolvedRouteTarget` for the next attempt. There is no "preserve resolved target through dwell" rule; dwell timing lives in `task_runs[dwell_task_index].arrived_at`, not in the resolved target.

- `RouteRun` is the canonical per-train runtime object for one assigned route. Current-code implementation may still name the class `RouteExecutionState`, but every changed contract in this section refers to that object as the train's `RouteRun`.
- Keep `current_waypoint_index`, `target_waypoint_index`, `direction`, `route_version_seen`, and `task_runs`. Remove `arrived_at_platform_time`, `dwell_target`, and `last_waypoint_platform_id`; their data lives in `RouteRun.task_runs[i]`.
- `RouteRun.current_route_status` is route-owned temporary per-run status, not a shared route-definition cache. `RouteExecutor.handle_path_completion(...)` must read this object and the dispatcher-passed completion context only; it must not do fresh target resolution after dispatcher state has already changed. `RouteExecutor.handle_train_advanced(...)` also starts from this object when an armed target exists. If no target is currently armed, the callback may use pure route-mode next-waypoint lookup/resolution transiently to identify the current waypoint pointer for this one reported block advance, but it must not continue onward to consume a second waypoint from the same callback.
- `RouteRun.task_runs[target_waypoint_index].resolved_target` is the minimum route-owned callback context that must survive until that planning chain is consumed or explicitly discarded. `RouteExecutor._arm_next_target(...)` is the only ordinary planning/reconciliation helper that writes this field. Callback-time logic must not recover or replace it by writing a newly resolved target after shared runtime state has already changed; Phase-3 head-advance callbacks may only clear the consumed entry for the current reported movement.
- `RouteExecutor` owns a scheduling registry over these `RouteRun` entries. On each Phase 1 tick it iterates runnable runs in descending current shared `Route.priority` order rather than arbitrary per-train order. Among equal priorities, stable existing iteration order is acceptable in v1.
- Add `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(train_id, reason)` as the canonical route-owned removal entry point used by controller power-off, operator/admin route abort, and other train-removal flows. It performs the full removal sequence:
  - if a live dispatcher path still exists for this train (`active_path_id_by_train[train_id]` is set), the caller must already have cancelled it through `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` before invoking this method (the canonical caller is the same Phase-3 cleanup pass that already runs the cancel step, so the latch-cancel-then-callback flow handles this naturally),
  - if the controller is currently in `RUNNING_AT_STATION` (the `RouteRun` is dwelling), the method calls `TrainController.finish_dwell()` to exit dwell into the state-machine-derived stopped ready state before clearing route-run state,
  - it unassigns the train from any active route, removes `RouteExecutor.execution_states[train_id]`, and clears route-owned temporary planning/target state for that run.
  An explicit operator/admin "abort route" request is delivered through the controller-owned Phase-3 latch `pending_route_abort`; if a live path exists, the request also writes `pending_cancel_request` with a diagnostic `cancellation_kind = OPERATOR_CANCELLED`. That same Phase 3 cleanup calls `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(train_id, reason="operator_cancel")` only when the abort latch is consumed after the dispatcher cancel step (or the dispatcher-cancel-skipped no-active-path branch) has run. Aborting a dwelling train therefore takes that same path: Phase 3 cancels any live path (or skips the dispatcher cancel when none exists), then `remove_train_route_run(...)` exits dwell via `finish_dwell()` and removes the `RouteRun`.
- Do not add a synchronous `RouteExecutor.continue_route_run_after_eb(...)` hook. EB release is handled through latches: `TrainController.release_emergency_cmd()` writes `eb_released = True` and `departure_confirmation_required = True` (see Latch Table); the same train's later Phase 3 stopped-recovery cleanup consumes both the `path_cancelled` and `eb_released` latches when the controller has reached `IDLE` or `STANDBY`, clears `DEVIATED` when a `RouteRun` exists, and leaves the next leg unarmed. The next `RouteExecutor._execute_route_for_train(...)` tick then reruns the ordinary planning kernel from the current physical position by calling `_advance_past_covered_waypoints(...)`, the Phase-1 target-arm step (`_arm_next_target(train_id)`), and a brand-new `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)`; this flow does not reuse, complete, or preserve the cancelled pre-EB path chain.
- Initialize both task cursors to `-1` before the first completed task for every route run, including one-off operator/local routes. There is no "synthetic current-location start stop" branch: a one-off reposition route is authored as a single-task `stop` route; `current_waypoint_index = -1` means "no task has been completed yet" and `Route.tasks[0]` is the first planning target. There is no invariant that `target_waypoint_index` must be numerically greater than or equal to `current_waypoint_index`; on reverse round-trip legs the armed target may be numerically smaller than the last completed task.
- `current_waypoint_index` is the route-progress cursor: it always marks the last task the train has actually finished.
- `target_waypoint_index` is the pending-planning cursor: it marks the task the current live planning chain is presently targeting for the next point-to-point create/extend step on the current directed route walk. It may be numerically greater than `current_waypoint_index` on forward legs or numerically less on reverse legs. When no next task on the current directed route walk is armed beyond current progress, `target_waypoint_index == current_waypoint_index`.
- `-1` is a sentinel only. It means "no task has been completed yet" and must never be used as a Python-style negative index into `Route.tasks`.
- The initial `RouteRun.direction` is `"forward"` through the stored route order. The route plan remains an ordered list in v1. Reversible round-trip behavior comes only from `RouteRun.direction` flipping between `"forward"` and `"reverse"` plus the pure route-mode next-task helper inside `_arm_next_target(train_id)`, not from building a second linked or reversed route structure.
- When a new leg is armed or refreshed, the Phase-1 `_arm_next_target(train_id)` step writes `target_waypoint_index`, `direction`, and `RouteRun.task_runs[i].resolved_target` atomically. The resolved task is the canonical target for that planning decision; route planning must not skip beyond it in one pathfinder request.
- `RouteExecutor` resolves a concrete `ResolvedRouteTarget` on demand during planning. Once that result is adopted for the active attempt, `_arm_next_target(train_id)` writes it into `RouteRun.task_runs[target_waypoint_index].resolved_target` rather than leaving callbacks to re-resolve it later. This per-task resolved target may be rewritten on later retries, reconciliation, or retargeting only when the previous `create_path(...)` or `extend_path(...)` attempt failed to create or grow authority, when `extend_path(...)` returned `success=True/result_kind=blocked_no_growth`, or when cancellation/route-edit reconciliation cleared the prior attempt. Once `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` or `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` succeeds, the live `Path.requested_destination_block_id`, `Path.requested_destination_direction`, and `Path.authority_end_block_id` remain the dispatcher-owned stored target contract for that successful segment, while `task_runs[target_waypoint_index].resolved_target` remains the route-owned callback reference for that segment until the task advances.
- `target_waypoint_index` rollback applies when `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` fails without growing authority while a live path still exists, and when `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(...)` consumes a matching live-path cancellation. In those cases, `RouteExecutor` keeps physical progress unchanged, clears `task_runs[target_waypoint_index].resolved_target` for the interrupted planning attempt, sets `target_waypoint_index = current_waypoint_index`, and lets a later Phase 1 call `_arm_next_target(train_id)` from the current shared route, current physical progress, and the current live authority end or no-authority stopped position as applicable. `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` failure does not trigger this rollback because no live path exists yet and the current `target_waypoint_index` is already the first index to retry.
- `RouteExecutor` reads the live path id via `TrainController.received_path_plan.path_id` (or `None` when no plan is pushed). A single live path may remain active across multiple task segments, but `_arm_next_target(train_id)` arms exactly one task at a time. After `waypoint` consumption, `dwell` completion, `stop` completion, or skip, `task_runs[i].resolved_target` for the consumed task is cleared, `target_waypoint_index` is reset to `current_waypoint_index`, and only a later progress callback or later Phase 1 may discover the next task on the same `path_id` when authority is still live. The same `RouteExecutor.handle_train_advanced(...)` callback never consumes more than one task.
- Keep a pre-departure retry flag such as `waiting_for_path` only for "path request failed before movement began" handling.
- Set `waiting_for_path = True` only when pre-departure platform lookup, current-block lookup, or dispatcher path creation fails before any authority exists.
- Do not use `waiting_for_path` for a live best-effort truncated path. That case still has active authority; the route should keep the same path alive and retry `extend_path()` on it rather than dropping into the pre-authority failure bucket.
- Clear `waiting_for_path` on successful path creation, on authoritative path completion, and when `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(...)` consumes a cancellation notification for the route's current live path.
- Do not add a separate controller operational sub-state for cancellation recovery in v1. When a live path is cancelled, `PathDispatcher` still clears controller authority and the controller enters the no-authority braking path against the current occupied-block boundary. The deferred recovery gate is the `path_cancelled` latch (see Latch Table); `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(...)` sets it and records diagnostic context immediately in Phase 3, but does not choose a new target or create fresh authority there. Once the train reaches the stopped no-authority state, the same train's Phase 3 stopped-recovery cleanup consumes `path_cancelled`, clears `DEVIATED`, and leaves the next leg unarmed. A later eligible Phase 1 then re-enters ordinary covered-task normalization plus `_arm_next_target(train_id)` from the train's current physical position.
- Add one route-owned covered-task normalization helper `RouteExecutor._advance_past_covered_waypoints(train, route, exec_state)`. It calls `SimulationController.get_or_create_controller(train_id).get_current_span()` and inspects the next task on the current directed route walk from the confirmed progress cursor. With the initial sentinel state `current_waypoint_index == -1` and forward initial direction, the first call yields task `0`. For a station/platform task it must first resolve the current attempt's concrete target block before applying the coverage test, except that a `task_kind == "waypoint"` station/platform task with `flexible_platform = True` is already satisfied when the train currently covers any compatible concrete platform/block in that task's allowed station/platform set. In that case, `RouteExecutor` uses the actually covered compatible platform/block only for the coverage decision and optional diagnostics. If the next task is `task_kind == "waypoint"` and its resolved target block is already physically covered by the train's current span, the task is treated as already passed and `_advance_past_covered_waypoints(...)` advances past it by calling `RouteExecutor._skip_waypoint(...)` immediately without requesting a same-block `create_path(...)`. If the next task is `task_kind == "stop"`, block coverage alone is never enough to skip or finish it. An unserved stop remains the current target until dispatcher-committed stop completion finishes it; if its resolved target block is already covered, Phase 1 must arm the stop and request or keep same-block stop authority. If the next task is `task_kind == "dwell"`, it is handled separately by the dwell-pending branch, not by coverage skip. After each `waypoint` skip, the helper re-inspects the next task from the updated progress cursor rather than incrementing the task index numerically.

**Canonical Route-Progress Events**

- `current_waypoint_index` may advance only from these route-owned events in v1:
  - head-based `task_kind == "waypoint"` consumption in `RouteExecutor.handle_train_advanced(...)`, at most one task per head-advance callback
  - `task_kind == "dwell"` completion after the dwell timer elapses following a dispatcher-committed stop arrival
  - explicit skip handling such as already-covered `waypoint` normalization or post-cancellation covered-task normalization after any recoverable cancellation
  - route edit / reconciliation that explicitly declares one or more pending tasks skipped
- Head-based `task_kind == "waypoint"` consumption is a route-owned in-motion progress event, not a dispatcher stop-completion event. The canonical trigger is the progress chain `TrainPhysics.tick(...)` -> `TrainController.on_physics_updated(event_time, span_update)` -> `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` -> `RouteExecutor.handle_train_advanced(...)`. `RouteExecutor.handle_train_advanced(train_id, path_id, resource_type, resource_id, resource_traversal_direction=None)` must first compare the callback's `path_id` with `PathDispatcher.active_path_id_by_train[train_id]`. If they differ, latch `TrainController.pending_cancel_request` with `cancellation_kind = PathCancellationKind.REVOKED` and `expected_path_id = path_id`, record diagnostics, and return without consuming any task. If `resource_type != "block"`, the route side does no task completion. For a block entry, `RouteExecutor.handle_train_advanced(...)` compares the callback's one reported block/direction with the current next task pointer on the current directed route walk. That pointer is `RouteRun.task_runs[target_waypoint_index].resolved_target` when an armed target exists; otherwise the callback may transiently read `Route.tasks[current_waypoint_index + offset]` to identify the current task. If the reported physical advance finishes or passes the current task, consume exactly that one task: set `exec_state.current_waypoint_index = task_index`, set `exec_state.direction = target.direction`, record `task_runs[task_index].arrived_at` and `task_runs[task_index].finished_at` as appropriate for the task kind, clear `task_runs[task_index].resolved_target`, set `target_waypoint_index = current_waypoint_index`, and return. It must not continue to compare the same advance with the following task, must not call `_arm_next_target(...)`, must not create or extend authority, and must not treat the callback as future planning. It does not stop the train, does not clear dispatcher authority, and does not call `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()`. `RouteExecutor.update(...)` and `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` must not re-derive `waypoint` consumption by polling controller span/lookahead state.
- `RouteExecutor._skip_waypoint(train, route, exec_state, task_index, direction, reason)` is the single route-owned skip helper for already-covered `waypoint` normalization, route edit/reconciliation, explicit operator skip, and post-cancellation covered-task normalization after any recoverable cancellation. It records physical route progress without dwell: set `exec_state.current_waypoint_index = task_index`, set `exec_state.direction = direction`, clear `task_runs[task_index].resolved_target`, set `target_waypoint_index = current_waypoint_index`, do not call `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()`, and do not synthesize dispatcher completion. The helper does NOT call `_arm_next_target(...)`, create authority, or commit next-leg planning. It only updates the route cursor and returns. After each skip the caller may continue the same eligible Phase 1 pass from the train's current physical position, re-run task normalization, and then arm the first task whose `waypoint` coverage or completed-stop/dwell status has not already resolved it on that same tick. If the skip was caused by a live-path cancellation, that cancellation must already have been committed in an earlier Phase 3 by `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(...)`; the next eligible Phase 1 then uses the ordinary correction-before-planning flow without branching on cancellation kind.
- `target_waypoint_index` is only a planning cursor. It may advance when the Phase-1 target-arm step arms a new target, when route reconciliation retargets the run, or when physical route progress updates `current_waypoint_index` through `RouteExecutor.handle_train_advanced(...)`, dwell completion for a stop, or `RouteExecutor._skip_waypoint(...)` and the planning cursor is then reset to match that newly advanced progress. A successful `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` or `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` that merely causes live authority to reach the currently armed waypoint target does not by itself advance `target_waypoint_index`; v1 still waits for physical pass-through consumption, dwell completion for a stop, or explicit skip. A best-effort create/extend that grows `resource_sequence` but leaves the live authority end short of the current target likewise keeps the same `target_waypoint_index`. `target_waypoint_index` never proves physical progress by itself.

**Route-executor state reads**

- `RouteExecutor` should read live operational state from `TrainController`, not from mirrored runtime fields on `Train`.
- Departure, retry, arrival, dwell, and waiting decisions should use controller state as the operational source of truth.
- `Train` may still expose persisted or UI-facing state, but route-control decisions should not depend on it being a live mirror.
- In the desired model, manual driving is `control_mode == manual`, not a separate operational state. A route-managed manual train still uses ordinary `STANDBY` / `IDLE` / `RUNNING_*` states and dispatcher authority; the difference from ATO is how movement begins and is driven, not whether `RouteExecutor` owns the waypoint plan. In particular, manual `IDLE`/`STANDBY` must not block route-side path creation. It changes only what `depart()` means after authority exists.

**Planning Kernel**

- Phase 1 is a state-machine: each canonical step has explicit preconditions and is a no-op when those preconditions are not met. The kernel never creates fresh authority while the train has not yet reached a stopped recovery point, while any teardown/recovery latch is still dirty, while the controller is `OFF`, or while the `RouteRun` is pending/inside a dwell step.
- Eligibility precondition for the planning kernel: `RouteExecutor` may run steps 3–5 (arm next target, resolve, and call `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` or `extend_path(...)`) only when one of the following is true:
  - the train has live authority (`TrainController.received_path_plan is not None`), in which case only step 5's `extend_path(...)` branch is allowed; or
  - the train has no live authority AND `TrainController.state` is `IDLE` or `STANDBY` AND the handoff state is clean, in which case the create branch is allowed.
- Clean handoff state for the no-live-authority create/replan branch means `TrainController.pending_cancel_request is None`, `TrainController.pending_route_abort is None`, `TrainController.pending_power_off == False`, `TrainController.eb_active == False`, and `RouteRun.path_cancelled == False` (see Latch Table). A `RouteRun.current_route_status.state == "NO_PATH"` marker is still clean for retry; the next eligible Phase 1 clears it back to `RUNNING` before retrying. A diagnostic `DEVIATED` marker is not itself a gate, but it is reset by the same Phase 3 stopped-recovery cleanup that consumes `path_cancelled`.
- If the controller is `OFF`, Phase 1 is skipped. If eligibility preconditions are otherwise unmet (still braking, dirty handoff), Phase 1 returns and waits for a later tick.
- For the EB case, the same eligibility rule applies: while `eb_active == True`, the controller is not in `IDLE` / `STANDBY` and Phase 1 produces no authority. After `release_emergency_cmd()` returns the controller to `IDLE` or `STANDBY`, the next eligible Phase 1 runs the planning kernel normally.
- One route-planning pass has five canonical steps in v1:
  1. Inspect `Route.tasks[next_task_index]` (from `_arm_next_target(train_id)` logic). If `task_kind == "dwell"`, treat dwell as the active route step. If the controller is in `IDLE` or `STANDBY`, write `TrainController.dwell_entry_requested` and return for that tick; the next Phase-2 controller tick consumes that request by running the `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()` acceptance logic. If the controller is already `RUNNING_AT_STATION`, return. Do not arm a next target or create/extend authority while dwell is pending or active.
  2. Normalize current physical position through `RouteExecutor._advance_past_covered_waypoints(...)`. Path planning does not arm or request authority until this correction pass has advanced past every already-covered `waypoint` task that qualifies for skip under the task-kind rules; unserved `stop` tasks remain eligible targets even when their resolved block is already covered.
  3. Arm or refresh the next pending task through `_arm_next_target(train_id)` when no next task on the current directed route walk is already armed, when the prior attempt was cleared, or when shared-route reconciliation requires a refresh.
  4. Resolve exactly one task into one `ResolvedRouteTarget` and write it into `task_runs[target_waypoint_index].resolved_target`.
  5. Call `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` or `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` for that one resolved task only.
- A single Phase 1 pass may loop locally through these steps only while it remains inside Phase-1-owned procedures. Same-tick retries are allowed for immediate results such as target selection, candidate re-selection, lock acquisition rollback, and another `create_path(...)` / `extend_path(...)` attempt on the same planning problem. For one planning problem, Phase 1 may try multiple distinct same-tick resolved targets only when the current task is a logical `station_id` task: `RouteExecutor` builds one ordered transient candidate list of all compatible available platform-end `(requested_destination_block_id, requested_destination_direction)` pairs for that station, sorted by the canonical graph comparison, then tries each distinct pair at most once in that Phase 1 pass. A concrete `platform_id` task, direct `block_id` task, or explicit local-reposition task has only one resolved pair for that tick; if that create/extend attempt fails without creating or growing authority, the run waits until a later eligible tick rather than trying a second end or platform in the same pass. `task_runs[target_waypoint_index].resolved_target` holds only the one candidate currently being attempted; the rest of the station candidate list is transient Phase-1 state and is not persisted. If every distinct same-tick station candidate fails without authority creation or growth and no live path exists for that run, `RouteExecutor` must set `RouteRun.current_route_status.state = NO_PATH`, clear `task_runs[target_waypoint_index].resolved_target`, set `target_waypoint_index = current_waypoint_index`, and stop Phase 1 for that train for that tick. On the next eligible Phase 1, `NO_PATH` clears back to `RUNNING` before retrying the same planning problem. If a live path still exists and `extend_path(...)` returns `success=True/result_kind=blocked_no_growth`, keep `RouteRun.current_route_status.state = RUNNING`, preserve `active_path_id`, leave the live path in place, and retry on later eligible ticks. If `extend_path(...)` returns `success=False/failure_kind=no_viable_path`, `invalid_reverse`, or another non-temporary structural failure for that live path, `RouteExecutor` must latch cancellation and wait for the ordinary Phase 3 cancel/recovery flow before any fresh create-path attempt.
- `RouteExecutor` never asks dispatcher/pathfinder to solve a whole multi-task route in one search. Longer authorities are formed only by repeated point-to-point segments appended onto the same live path identified by `path_id`.
- In normal route-managed execution, v1 does not concatenate beyond the currently armed task on the same tick. Route planning arms exactly one task at a time. If the current armed task is `task_kind == "waypoint"`, that task is still consumed only by the head-entry callback chain; after that physical consumption, the next Phase 1 tick may resolve the next task and extend or recreate authority from the train's then-current physical state.
- A waypoint-terminated authority before that next planning tick is a degraded but valid v1 authority boundary. If the train later brakes there, that is a safety stop caused by lack of further authority, not a planned stop for the `waypoint` task itself.
- `task_kind == "stop"` versus `task_kind == "waypoint"` changes only what happens when the resolved target is reached: `stop` enters dwell (or the `dwell` task that follows it) through dispatcher-committed completion from the post-physics controller stop check, while `waypoint` advances immediately without dwell. It does not change the dispatcher path API shape.

**Route Edit And Reconciliation**

- Active runs reference the current shared `Route` through `RouteRun.route_id`; later edits to that shared route are live and must increment `Route.route_version`.
- The Phase-1 target-arm step is the canonical reconciliation entry point. When `Route.route_version != RouteRun.route_version_seen`, it compares the current run cursor and armed target against the edited shared route and applies `Route.reconciliation_policy`: `nearest_valid_target` keeps the current route-side cursor when still valid and otherwise chooses the valid next target whose resolved target is graph-nearest to the train's current physical span / progress cursor on the network, not the numerically nearest waypoint index; ties prefer preserving current directed-walk progress and then current route order. `restart_from_top` resets the directed walk to the first waypoint on the active route mode. Reconciliation is route-side only: after it chooses the new task/cursor/direction, it discards any previously adopted concrete platform/block resolution and reruns ordinary target resolution from current physical progress under the edited shared route. `nearest_valid_target` therefore does not preserve an old `ResolvedRouteTarget` or old concrete platform choice.
- Operator retargeting and route swap use the same mechanism: `RouteExecutor` changes the run's `route_id` to a different shared route definition (for direct retargets, a one-off `RouteMode.ONE_OFF` route built from the train's current location plus the requested destination, where the synthetic start is a direct `block_id` resolved from the head-side cab block, or for no-cab from the current-span end whose graph distance to the destination terminal block is shortest under the canonical comparison order). After the `route_id` swap, the retargeted run uses the ordinary Phase 1 planning kernel like any other run.
- If a live path exists when the run's `route_id` changes or shared-route reconciliation invalidates the future target chain, Phase 1 latches `pending_cancel_request` (`OPERATOR_CANCELLED` for operator-driven changes; `REVOKED` for system/non-operator changes) and waits for the ordinary Phase 3 cancel-and-replan flow. There is no same-tick progress jump or in-place path replacement.

**Concrete `RouteExecutor` tick calls**

- `RouteExecutor.update(delta_time)` should continue to increment `RouteExecutor.simulation_time` for simulation bookkeeping and refresh route-managed scheduling inputs for this tick. `RouteExecutor` owns the final runnable-train ordering through `RouteExecutor.order_runnable_trains(candidate_train_ids)`: every candidate train resolves to one effective priority (route-managed trains use the current shared `Route.priority`; trains without a current `RouteRun` use effective priority `5`), and the merged set is sorted strictly by effective priority descending (`9` highest, `0` lowest) with stable existing iteration order as the only tie-break. Route-managed and non-route trains at the same effective priority interleave in stable iteration order; route management does not bump priority within a tier. `SimulationController.update(...)` owns executing the returned train IDs, but it must not apply a second priority sort. `RouteExecutor.update(delta_time)` prepares the route-managed Phase 1 work that `SimulationController` will invoke by train; it must not itself call `RouteExecutor._execute_route_for_train(...)` for every train in a world-wide pass. Trains without a current `RouteRun` still remain in the `SimulationController` loop and simply have no route-managed Phase 1 work for that tick. Stored route/run timestamps such as `Route.started_at`, `RouteRun.task_runs[i].arrived_at`, `RouteRun.error_time`, and `RouteTask.target_time` use system-time Unix seconds.
- `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(train_id, delta_time)` is the entire Phase 1 route-executor tick for one train. `SimulationController.update(...)` calls it immediately before that same train's `SimulationController._update_train(train, delta_seconds)` transaction. The method may delegate to an implementation helper such as `RouteExecutor._execute_route_for_train(...)`, but the changed contract is owned by this route-executor tick: dwell entry, covered-waypoint normalization, target arming, station candidate iteration, and `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` / `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` attempts all live here.
- The canonical public Phase-1 surface is `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(train_id, delta_seconds)`. `_execute_route_for_train(...)` is a private implementation helper inside that surface and is not referenced by any cross-component contract; downstream sections refer to `tick_train_phase1`. `tick_train_phase1` creates or reuses the train's `RouteRun` (current-code class may still be named `RouteExecutionState`). If the controller is `OFF`, Phase 1 route work is skipped and power-on is handled by the Phase-2 command slot. If the next task on the route walk has `task_kind == "dwell"` and `task_runs[dwell_index].finished_at is None`, it treats that dwell task as the current route step and returns without planning after any required dwell-entry command. Otherwise, when `target_waypoint_index == current_waypoint_index`, when the prior attempt was cleared, or when `Route.route_version != exec_state.route_version_seen`, it runs `_arm_next_target(train_id)` before any create/extend decision.
- Before any create/extend decision, `RouteExecutor._execute_route_for_train(...)` must ensure that `task_runs[target_waypoint_index].resolved_target` was most recently written by `_arm_next_target(train_id)` for the current shared route and current physical progress. If that step cannot currently resolve a legal concrete platform/block target under the task's fixed/flexible platform policy, the retry fails at target resolution and `RouteExecutor` must not call `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` for that tick.
- Before any create/extend decision, the current attempt's resolved target is the route-owned request contract for `target_waypoint_index`: the task target block and the route-owned `requested_destination_direction` constraint. It must not resolve or pass any stop-position centimeter field. Dispatcher and controller use the chosen terminal frame plus controller safety margin for braking/boundary projection.
- Route-managed path building is always segment-by-segment along the current directed route walk. On each create/extend attempt, the resolved target represents exactly one resolved task target, never a bundled multi-task search request. After one segment is successfully created or appended, route planning stops for that train on that tick; if the armed target is `task_kind == "waypoint"`, the next task is not armed until that `waypoint` is physically consumed and the next planning tick runs.
- `RouteExecutor._execute_route_for_train(...)` should ensure the train's `TrainController` exists before it reads controller state or calls `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)`. In the desired model a route-managed train should already have a controller by the time authority is assigned in Phase 1. `SimulationController` owns only the 1:1 binding between `train_id` and `TrainController`; it does not construct or push path plans.
- Before arming a new pending task or retrying the current one, `RouteExecutor._execute_route_for_train(...)` should normalize current progress through `RouteExecutor._advance_past_covered_waypoints(...)` so the route does not keep targeting a task the train already physically covers.
- The operational state checks inside `RouteExecutor._execute_route_for_train(...)` should read controller state rather than `train.state`.
- If controller state is `OFF`, `RouteExecutor._execute_route_for_train(...)` is not invoked for normal Phase 1 planning. A powered-off train must not create, extend, or rely on dispatcher authority, and the system must not auto-power-on it. `TrainController.power_on_cmd()` is accepted only through the Phase-2 command slot; route planning resumes on a later tick after the controller is powered and in an eligible state.
